### Inside Front Cover

The inside front cover features a vibrant display of **marbled paper**. This decorative style was created by floating oil-based pigments on a bath of thickened water, swirling them into patterns, and then laying the paper on top to "pick up" the design. This particular pattern consists of intricate swirls of blue, grey, yellow, and red.

In the center of the marbled paper is a circular <term>bookplate</term> <note>Also known as an "ex-libris," these were custom-printed labels pasted into books to identify the owner.</note>.

->From the library of Walter Sneyd<-
<note>original: "Ex libris Gualteri Sneyd"</note>

This bookplate identifies a previous owner of the manuscript, **Walter Sneyd** (1809–1888), a famous English clergyman and collector of rare books. The plate depicts a <term>heraldic crest</term>—a traditional family symbol—consisting of:

*   A helmet topped by a **lion rampant** <note>A lion shown standing on its hind legs with its forepaws raised, a common symbol of strength and nobility.</note>.
*   A shield below the helmet featuring a **fleur-de-lis** <note>A stylized lily or iris used as a decorative and heraldic symbol.</note>.

The presence of this plate provides <term>provenance</term>, allowing us to trace the journey of this manuscript through the hands of significant historical collectors.

<summary>This page shows the inside front cover of the volume, featuring decorative marbled paper and the heraldic bookplate of the renowned 19th-century collector Walter Sneyd.</summary>
<keywords>Walter Sneyd, ex-libris, bookplate, heraldry, marbled paper, provenance, book history, lion rampant, fleur-de-lis</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the legal authorization, or "Privilege," for the printing of Claude Prieur’s work. It follows the title page, which established the book's subject (lycanthropy) and the author's credentials. This text serves as a formal copyright notice from the Spanish Habsburg authorities who ruled the Southern Netherlands at the time.</meta>

## Extract of the Privilege.

BY special grace and Privilege of the King, J. Masius, <term>Sworn Printer</term> <note>A printer who took an oath to the crown or a university to follow censorship and quality laws in exchange for exclusive rights.</note> to His Majesty, is permitted to print a certain book entitled, *Dialogue on Lycanthropy or the Transformation of Men into Wolves, and whether such a thing can happen.* Composed by Friar Claude Prieur, of the order of Saint Francis, and inspected and approved by Messieurs Henricus Cuyckius, Bishop-elect of Roermond, and by Friar Gilles Cheheré, Doctor and Professor in Holy Theology. With express prohibition to all Printers, Booksellers, and merchants against printing or having printed, selling, or distributing the said Book throughout all his Territories of Brabant and <term>Overmaas</term> <note>original: "oultre Meuze." These were the "lands beyond the Meuse River," a collection of territories associated with the Duchy of Brabant.</note>, for the space of six years, counting from the day and date of the printing of the aforementioned book, under penalty of confiscation of the said books and an arbitrary fine, as is more fully contained in the letters of the said Privilege. Given at Brussels, the first day of December, 1595.

->Undersigned<-

->**De Buschere.**<-

<summary>This page presents the official royal privilege granted in 1595, giving Jean Maes the exclusive right to print Prieur's treatise on werewolves for six years. It names the theological censors who approved the work's content, ensuring it aligned with Catholic doctrine.</summary>

<keywords>Privilege, copyright, Jean Maes, Henricus Cuyckius, Brabant, Louvain, book history, censorship, 1595</keywords>

# TO THE GENTLEMEN
## THE PASTOR, BAILLIE,
### ALDERMEN AND MAGISTRACY OF WAVRE,

->Humble Greetings.<-

<image-desc>An ornamental drop-cap letter 'M' featuring a cherub or putto figure seated within the decorative frame of the letter.</image-desc>Gentlemen, knowing how much ingratitude is dismissed and, by every well-born soul, rejected for the infinity of vicious roots that it drags behind it; knowing, on the contrary, how much the remembrance and <term>repayment</term> <note>original: "reuenche." In this context, it refers to returning a favor or a "tit-for-tat" of kindness rather than vengeance.</note> of a good deed is cherished and prized for the marvelous grace they possess in maintaining a mutual friendship as well as all other moral virtues; hearing, on the other hand, that poet <note>original: "Martial." Marcus Valerius Martialis (c. 38–103 AD), a Roman poet famous for his short, biting poems called epigrams.</note> Martial shouting at the top of his lungs that it is a "shameful blush" <note>original: "rougeur hōteuse." This is an English translation of Martial's sentiment that it is embarrassing to always receive gifts without giving anything back.</note> to always take and give nothing; I have already, during the two years that I have lived among you, sought to find an opportunity to reciprocate so many of your liberalities and almost infinite courtesies, shown at all times both to my predecessors, your preachers, and to me, being very well known and experienced from day to day.

<meta>catchword: But (original: "Mais")</meta>

<summary>Friar Claude Prieur begins his dedication to the local leaders of Wavre, expressing his deep gratitude for their hospitality and support over the past two years. He justifies the publication of his book as a moral obligation to "repay" their kindness, citing the Roman poet Martial to emphasize that it is shameful to receive without giving.</summary>

# EPISTLE.

But alas! What could he do or undertake who is poorer than <note>Job: A biblical figure known for losing all his wealth and health, becoming an archetype of suffering and poverty.</note> Job, more destitute than <note>Bias of Priene: One of the "Seven Sages of Greece," famous for the saying "Omnia mea mecum porto" (All that is mine, I carry with me).</note> Bias or <note>Diogenes: Diogenes the Cynic, who famously lived in a ceramic jar and owned nothing but a cloak, a staff, and a bread bag.</note> Diogenes, more deprived than <note>Aristides: Known as "Aristides the Just," an Athenian statesman who was so honest that he died in such poverty that the state had to pay for his funeral.</note> Aristides ever was? Nevertheless, on one hand, I heard the outcry against ingratitude; on the other, I recognized my own <term>weakness</term> <note>original: "debilté." Here meaning lack of financial or material resources.</note>; and on yet another—which was most distressing to me—I heard <note>Hesiod: An early Greek poet; the author is referencing a passage from Hesiod's *Works and Days* regarding the repayment of favors.</note> Hesiod echoing to me that a benefit received is like <term>seed</term> cast into the ground, from which one ought to harvest more than was sown. Indeed, this dilemma was no small burden to me when, having good will, I was destitute of means; and with good reason I might have called myself one who, having nimble wings on his right arm, seems ready to fly promptly, were he not held back by the heavy burden hanging from his <term>left arm</term> <note>original: "bras senestre." This is a common Renaissance emblem or metaphor for high ambition or talent being weighed down by poverty.</note>. 

Nevertheless, turning the other way and remembering your good inclination toward me, as well as the axiom of the great orator <note>Cicero: Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and philosopher. Prieur refers to Cicero’s view that a debt of gratitude is repaid by the very act of feeling grateful, even if material repayment is impossible.</note> Cicero—releasing and excusing those who, being unable to fully satisfy an obligation, nonetheless demonstrate good will—I began to resolve my doubt. Presuming upon your accustomed kindness, I thought that in offering you this small labor of mine, you would not look upon its smallness or low value

<meta>catchword: thus (original: "ainsi")</meta>

<summary>Prieur describes his struggle to repay the town's kindness, contrasting his poverty with the high expectations of classical authors. He concludes that his sincere "good will" and his book should be accepted as a symbolic repayment.</summary>

# BROTHER AEGIDIUS CHEHERE
## Doctor of Sacred Theology and Professor in the Convent of the Friars Minor at Louvain <note>original: "Louanij." Now Leuven, Belgium; its university was a major center for Catholic orthodoxy during the Counter-Reformation.</note>, congratulates the author out of friendship with this poem.

therefore ho[nor]
frie[ndship]
[poem]
[congratulates]
Dam[ascene] <note>A potential reference to St. John Damascene, though unclear in this context.</note>
Ho[nor]
Offere[d]

Acts [of the neighbor]
Mocking p[ublicly]

*Claude, while you pursue your calm study under a prosperous divine power,*
*You offer a noble feast of your intellect, with which the mind,*
*Weary from leisure and wounded by injury, may refresh itself.*
*Hence, I extol you with deserved praise,*
*And I congratulate your studies with all my heart;*
*Wishing that posterity, with favorable prayers,*
*May duly receive this work of yours.*
*This will come to pass in an easy manner, provided that*
*Zoilus <note>A 4th-century BC Greek critic known for his bitter attacks on Homer; his name became a standard term for a malicious, nitpicking critic.</note> sets aside his rabid attacks—*
*He who always mocks the excellent deeds and acts of his neighbor*
*With wide, gaping jaws.*

# BROTHER JAMES GARNIER

## FRANCISCAN, TO THE
### Reader, an Hendecasyllabic Poem <note>original: "Hendecaſyllabon." A classical poetic meter of eleven syllables per line, often used in Latin literature for light, witty, or personal verses.</note>.

Turn your eyes here, friend Reader,
*Turn here a watchful and well-disposed mind,*
*For* **CLAUDE** <note>The author, Claude Prieur.</note> *celebrated with no small*
*reputation, my colleague*
*and pleasant friend, teaches you witty things*
*in this present little book (even if he does not*
*report places, deeds, and nations) <note>Garnier suggests that while the book may not be a geographical or historical travelogue, it contains intellectual wit and moral value.</note>.*
*Take these things up, and revolve them frequently in your mind,*
*Or remember to be fair and honest,*
*For you will not shudder by reading often,*
*If you bring a good and kind judgment.*

<margin>
James Garnier
Franciscan
Hendecasyllabic
[poem]
</margin>

# BROTHER RENÉ SANDRÉ
## OF LIÈGE <note>original: "Leodini." Liège is a city in modern-day Belgium, then part of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, A Poem to the Author.

*Just as our minds are gladly carried away into the wilds, when*
*the birds sing a sweet song from their slender throats;*
*And when dances and songs flourish, and throughout all the gardens*
*the generous earth blooms with fragrant roses;*
*So too are these things especially <note>original: "πρότερον" (*proteron*). A Greek term meaning "firstly," "primarily," or "before all else."</note> most pleasing to our souls,*
*which your labor gathers together with such excellent skill.*
*With you as our guide, the outrages dominating the whole world are revealed,*
*along with the torches of wickedness while war <note>original: "Marte furente," literally "while Mars rages." Mars was the Roman god of war.</note> rages.*
*With you as our guide, the evil origin of rebellion is shown,*
*and why these ages are soaked in such bitter gall.*
*How the son-in-law against his father-in-law, or the son against his parent, prepares for battle,*
*and how the witch <note>original: "saga." A Latin term for a female sorceress or fortune-teller, often used in a pejorative sense in early modern demonology.</note> does harm through her evil service.*
*While she summons hellish crowds with a magical shrieking,*
*and halts the flow of a rushing river with an incantation.*
*And when it pleases her, she drives the clouds from a rainy sky;*
*when it pleases her, she destroys limbs with harsh pain.*
*And how, when a phantom <note>original: "larua." This can mean a ghost, a mask, or a demonic spirit; here it refers to the deceptive appearance of a transformed human.</note> takes on a false shape,*
*though it is truly a man, it is seen to be a wolf <note>This refers to lycanthropy (werewolves), a central topic of Prieur's treatise.</note>.*
*Through you, the vain opinion of the ancients is exposed,*
*along with the whole story crafted with lying faith;*
*Those who believe that human souls return into foreign bodies*
*after drinking deep from the lakes of the Styx <note>The Styx was the river of the underworld in Greek mythology. This refers to the ancient doctrine of metempsychosis, or the transmigration of souls, which Prieur argues against.</note>.*
*If you, kind Reader, receive this labor with gratitude,*
*the author **Claude** rightly offers his thanks to you.*

<sig>AVX</sig>

<summary>In this poem, René Sandré praises Claude Prieur for exposing the "evil origins" of social unrest and the deceptions of witchcraft, specifically mentioning werewolves and the false belief in the reincarnation of souls.</summary>
<keywords>René Sandré, Claude Prieur, witchcraft, werewolves, lycanthropy, metempsychosis, Styx, magic incantations, demons</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which featured a Latin poem by René Sandré, this page presents a French sonnet by "I. G." (Jacques Garnier). Both men are members of the same religious order as the author, Claude Prieur, and their poems serve as endorsements for his work on lycanthropy and demonic transformations. This page carries the catchword "PRE-," indicating that the formal Preface follows.</meta>

# TO THE READERS
## SONNET.

You who have, through vain illusion,
Filled your hearts with such fantasies;
Whose souls have been seized by error,
And constrained by compelling <note>original: "pregnante raison." In this context, it refers to a logic that is forceful or convincing, yet perhaps misleading if based on false premises.</note> logic;

You must yield, and let the judgment
Of this Author <note>Referring to Claude Prieur, the writer of this treatise.</note> release and unbind you;
So that you may learn from another perspective, in part,
How such a transformation <term>transformation</term> <note>original: "mutation." This specifically refers to the shapeshifting or "metamorphosis" of humans into wolves, which Prieur argues is a diabolical illusion.</note> occurs.

This lofty discourse, this metamorphosis,
Has various matters enclosed within it.
A subject that, through its own variety,

Satisfies man, for nature desires it;
If then you wish to agree with what we say,
Why are you so slow to embrace this new work? <note>original: "nouueauté." While it means "novelty," it here refers to the newly published book and the fresh insights it provides into the mysteries of the supernatural.</note>

->I. G.<- <note>These initials belong to Jacques Garnier, a fellow Franciscan who provided this commendatory verse for his colleague's book.</note>

<summary>A commendatory sonnet addressed to the readers, urging them to set aside their preconceived notions and illusions about lycanthropy to learn the truth from Claude Prieur's discourse. The poem highlights the human desire for knowledge about the variety of nature and the supernatural.</summary>

# PREFACE
## TO THE READER.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring a bird, possibly a crane, standing amidst dense foliage and trees within a square border.</image-desc>Although it appeared to some in the past that social gatherings <note>The author uses "conuiues" here not just for the guests, but for the act of gathering itself.</note> and banquets were in themselves illicit, for certain reasons they were able to produce; nevertheless, the good and profit that very often result from them, and have resulted from them, together with the examples of those from whom we draw our inspiration, lead us much more readily to admit and maintain them than to reject them. For what is more useful and profitable (indeed, I say necessary), and also what is more recommended to us by both human and divine laws, than peace?

-> *peace, all-divine,* <-
-> *The daughter of friendship, worthy above all things.* <-

This is what we nevertheless acquire through this means. A witness to this for us will be that grave and renowned Cato, who, weighing and examining his words, not with an inconstant and variable judgment, has dared to call *the table the Mother and procreator of Friendship*. The same is shown to us by Plutarch, saying that <margin>Plutarch, Decade 1, Problem 4 <note>This refers to Plutarch's *Symposiacs* or *Table Talk*, a famous collection of dinner-table discussions.</note></margin> *a banquet is a way of living which, through the use of wine, ends in friendship under the allure of grace* <note>original Latin: "Conuiuium viuendi ratio est, quæ per vini vsum in amicitiam definit sub gratiæ lenocinio."</note>. The banquet <term>conuiue</term> <note>From the Latin "convivium." In this context, Prieur is defining the social and moral structure of shared meals as a foundation for civilization.</note> is a way of living which, through the use of wine, ends in friendship through an enticement of good grace. In another place, he introduces Thales the Wise, responding to a certain man named Alexidemus, who complained of having been placed at the foot of the table. <margin>The same in *The Banquet of the 7 Sages*</margin> *We do not sit down together,* he said, *to occupy the first seats, but so that we may accommodate ourselves to those sitting with us,*

# P R E F A C E

*...and by showing ourselves approachable, we offer them an immediate occasion and opportunity for forging a friendship among us.* <note>original Latin: "et faciles exhibentes occasionem eis & ansam præbeamus protinus faciende inter nos amicitie."</note>

We do not come to the table to take the highest rank, but rather for the purpose of accommodating those already seated; and by making ourselves easy-going, we give them the opportunity to quickly form a friendship. Cicero proves the same thing through the etymology of the name:

<margin>As Seneca says</margin> *“Our ancestors rightly called the banqueting-couch of friends a ‘convivium’ <note>original: conuiuium, from the Latin "convivere," meaning to live together. Prieur is highlighting that for the Romans, eating together was synonymous with living together.</note>, because it involved a shared union of life.”*

With good reason our ancestors called banquets the joyful gathering of friends, because they fostered a union of life. Alternatively, we may recall the laws of Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta (Lacedaemonia), credited with establishing the city's austere and communal social systems.</note>, and say that not only were the table and common feasts of the Spartans <note>original: Lacedemoniens</note> ordained to repress luxury and other vices by means of such an austere community, but even more so to make them equal in this way, in order to remove jealousy (which is contrary to charity and the mother of all vices), and so that just as they ate of the same bread, they should likewise be of the same accord and consent. The divine Plato, in providing the natural and essential distinction of humanity, did not forget to include this practice of banquets and sociable friendship (by which man makes himself known to be man), for which reason he said:

<margin>Marsilio Ficino in the commentary on Cicero’s Offices <note>Ficino was a celebrated Renaissance philosopher who translated Plato and commented on classical ethics.</note></margin> *“Humanity is threefold: the first occurs through greeting, as when we greet those we meet and extend a hand; the second when someone brings aid to one suffering in calamity; the third when men celebrate banquets among themselves and take pleasure in social interaction.”*

Humanity is proper to man, and can be proven in three ways; First, when meeting some—

someone, we give them our hand, saluting and welcoming them; secondly, when someone relieves and provides help to those in pain; thirdly, when men feast together in banquets and gatherings, they rejoice through this custom;

->*Thus, banquets celebrated together at a sweet table*<-
->*Produce an eternal pledge of friendship.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Sic celebrata simul dulci conuiuia mensa, Æterna generant pignus amicitiæ."</note>

Therefore, banquets being a means of joining us with such a bond of charity and friendship, we cannot cause offense by proposing such a goal before our eyes. Even if there have been, and are daily found, those who abuse them, these should not prejudice the others who will use them as is proper. I know that people will point out to me the murders of Herod, of Absalom, of a Roman Flaminius, and several others committed at the table <note>Herod Antipas famously ordered the decapitation of John the Baptist during a birthday feast (Matthew 14); Absalom ordered the murder of his brother Amnon during a sheep-shearing festival (2 Samuel 13); Lucius Quinctius Flamininus was expelled from the Roman Senate for executing a prisoner during a banquet to please a companion.</note>. I will be countered with the excess of drunkenness and brutality committed every day; the blasphemies, quarrels, slanders, dishonest words, and many scandals that arise from them will be placed before my eyes. But what of it? Is there any Sacrament <term>Sacrament</term> <note>A religious ceremony or act regarded as an outward and visible sign of inward and spiritual grace.</note>, virtue, or other good work that the devil or his followers do not abuse? And if the spider makes its ill profit from the juice of the flower, converting it into venom, shall it be said that the bee, taking from the same flower, does not turn it into sweet honey to draw substance and benefit from it? Furthermore, the goodness or badness of anything whatsoever is proven or rejected by the virtue or vice of those who have used such a thing for good or for evil, regardless of the multitude, but rather the quality of the persons who have used it. For certainly, if we wished to judge by the behavior and multitude of those who have exceeded, or by the number of the guests,

One finds a notable murder by Herod, the drunkenness of Alexander, the refinement of Heliogabalus, the gluttony of the rich man <note>A reference to the parable of the rich man (Dives) and Lazarus in Luke 16.</note>, and even of Cambles, King of Lydia <note>A legendary king of Lydia said to have suffered from such extreme gluttony that he ate his own wife while sleepwalking.</note>, the insolence of Nabal, the lust of Tiberius—having himself served by entirely naked girls—the excess of Nero, namely from noon until midnight, and of Vitellius, as much for his frequency and vileness as for the number of dishes, which reached nine thousand. If luxury is displayed, one sees an Octavius <note>The Emperor Augustus.</note> adorned like an Apollo, and all the guests like gods and goddesses. If numbers are considered, Alexander usually invited sixty or seventy; Claudius Caesar six hundred; Budé <note>Guillaume Budé (1467–1540), a famous French humanist scholar.</note> himself mentions some King of the Persians who invited fifteen thousand men. And truly these are marvelous things, and almost portents, that one reads regarding such and similar excesses. Therefore, we reject those people as men who, just as they were disordered and insolent in the rest of their actions, were the same in this regard. 

But when we enter into the remembrance of the virtuous assemblies and of good people mentioned in Holy Scripture and elsewhere (from whom we should rather take example), we find hospitality in the gatherings of Abraham and his nephew Lot; the fear of God in those of both the elder and younger Tobias; a modesty in that of Ahasuerus; the religiosity of the Egyptians, to whom a skull of a deceased person <note>A practice meant as a *memento mori*, reminding guests of their mortality amidst the feast.</note> was presented at the table; the sobriety of the prophets, the abstinence of the Saints; the laws <term>laws</term> <note>Specifically the "Table Laws" of St. Augustine, who famously had verses inscribed on his table forbidding gossip or slander against the absent.</note> of a Saint Augustine, the beautiful discourses full of charity and erudition in those of Jesus Christ our Savior. And even if all examples were lacking to us, that one alone should totally suffice for us: Let us not look to the preceding ones,

# TO THE READER.

To their excess or vice, by which every man of good character would be dissuaded from ever frequenting company; but instead let us cast our eyes upon our prototype and example—who is not inferior to Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta who instituted communal meals to promote social order.</note> or to those celebrated philosophers named above—who desired so much to decorate and honor banquets and feasts with his presence that the principal and most numerous miracles he performed were at the table. Sometimes he healed the sick, such as a man with dropsy <note>A historical term for edema, or the swelling of soft tissues due to the accumulation of excess water.</note>; at other times he converted sinners there, such as Mary Magdalene, Zacchaeus, and the tax collectors; on other occasions, he provided for the needs of his friends, as at the wedding feast <note>The Wedding at Cana, where Jesus turned water into wine.</note>. In short, his first and last miracles took place in such a setting. The last, I say, was not only on the day of his departure when he made his final farewell (as he withdrew his visible presence) to his Apostles while at the table on Mount Zion, but I also mean the final miracle he performed before his sacred Death and Passion. This banquet and supper was so famous and sumptuous, and the food in such great abundance, that among the dishes served there, the principal course—which is his Sacred Body and Blood—continues still, and we are invited to taste it when we wear that wedding garment <note>A reference to the parable of the wedding feast in Matthew 22, symbolizing a state of grace or purity.</note>, the robe of purity and innocence of life, and when we desire to draw near to it.

<margin>St. Thomas, Opusculum 57.</margin>
> "A precious and wonderful banquet, health-giving and filled with all sweetness, in which Christ, the true God, is offered to be received," <note>original: "Pratiosum & admirandū conuiuium, salutiferū, & omni suauitate repletū; in quo Christus sumēdus proponitur verus Deus."</note> says Saint Thomas; a precious and admirable banquet, salutary and full of all sweetness, where Jesus Christ, the true God, is offered to us to be received.

These are the benefits that come to us from gatherings and banquets when they are maintained primarily through edifying conversation, recognizing that these goods and liberalities come not only from the efforts of men, but from the largesse and goodness of our God.

# PREFACE

For the law of Christian civility <note>In this context, "civility" refers to the proper conduct and moral etiquette expected of a believer.</note> does not permit one to touch meat <note>The word "viandes" here refers generally to food or sustenance, not exclusively animal flesh.</note> without first acknowledging Him; rather as one unworthy of being driven from the table, following the teaching of Louys Vives <note>Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540), a prominent Spanish humanist and educational theorist who wrote extensively on social behavior.</note>.

<margin>Book on manners to be observed at the table.</margin>
> *Let no one take food unless a consecration is made,* <note>original: "Nemo cibum capiat, nisi consecratio fiat"</note>
> *Let him who scorns these teachings be deprived of the table.* <note>original: "Priuetur mensa, qui spreuerit haec documenta."</note>

And I believe with certainty that when a virtuous and honorable company comes together to refresh themselves joyfully without noise or scandal, with Christian modesty, in a conversation of friendship, having first sought God; that without doubt He is in their midst, inspiring them with discourse worthy of faith. I think this was recently the case in the company where we found ourselves, seeing that besides what is mentioned above, the host had not forgotten what is written:

<margin>Deuteronomy 14</margin>
> *Beware that you do not forsake the Levite who is within your gates.* <note>original: "Caue ne derelinquas leuitam qui intra portas tuas est." In the biblical context, this refers to providing for the religious tribe of Levi, who held no land of their own.</note>

Take care not to forget the Levite who is your neighbor; for certainly a third part of this company, composed of the number of the Graces <note>In Greek mythology, the three goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity.</note> and the Muses <note>The nine goddesses of the arts and sciences.</note>, belonged to God’s lot and portion. Because of this, it was impossible for God not to be present, considering what St. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquent preaching.</note> deduces on the subject:

<margin>Chrysostom, Sermon 24 on the Epistle to the Romans.</margin>
> *Jesus Christ will be present at your table, and will fill the entire banquet with his blessing, when you have prayed, when you have sung spiritual songs, when you have called the poor to share in the things served, and when you have ensured much discipline and modesty at your symposium.* <note>original: "Christus mensa vestra aderit, benedictioneq; sua totum conuiuium adimplebit, quando oraueris, quãdo spiritualia cecineris, quando pauperes ad eorum quae apposita sunt communionem vocaueris, quando multam symposio vestro disciplinam & modestiam procuraueris."</note>

Jesus Christ, he says, will be present at your table and will fill it with his blessing, provided that you have invoked him and sung his praises, and that you have called the poor to participate in what is served, provided that modesty and good discipline are among

First, the aforementioned <note>Referring to the classical authors Aulus Gellius or Varro introduced on the preceding page.</note> does not at all approve of somber or silent guests, saying that one must be joyful with talk not of doubtful questions <note>Topics that are overly academic, controversial, or obscure.</note>—which the whole company cannot understand—but of such matters as may be discussed in the public square. But, I ask you, what friendship can one find in a gloomy man with a furrowed and peevish brow, who seems to have some grievance or other to settle against someone? For as Socrates very well says: just as sour wine is to the taste, so too are troublesome and wild manners incompatible with company.

<margin>Stobaeus, *Sermones* 4, "On Imprudence."</margin>
*Neither is tart wine fit for drinking, nor are rustic manners fit for conversation.* <note>original: "Neque vinum auſterum eſt aptum potioni, neque mores agreſtes conuerſationi."</note> Plutarch considers the latter even harder to endure, saying that there is a remedy for the first, but not for the other. # TO THE READER.

For I know very well that the maxim of Diogenes <note>Likely referring to Diogenes Laërtius's *Lives of the Philosophers*.</note> in his first book is necessary: that although one must restrain the tongue in all places, this must be done above all at the table.

<margin>Seneca, *Epistle* 84.</margin>
I know furthermore the saying of Seneca in his 84th letter, that drunkenness is nothing other than a voluntary madness; but let rejoicing be of such a nature that sadness is excluded from it. For the aforementioned author, in order not to give too much rein to such recreation, immediately moderates his saying: *If especially,* he says, *they assemble lawfully and purely to live together, not entirely for the belly, but for the soul.* <note>original: "Si præsertim, legitimè, siq; castè coëant conuicturi, nec omnino ventri concanes, sed animæ."</note> This means: if they gather not so much joyful to fill their bellies as to refresh the soul. And he draws his reasoning from the very institution of banquets, which should be a joining together of a good life and a fellowship <term>communion</term> <note>From the Latin *communio*, implying a shared spiritual or social bond rather than just eating together.</note> rather than a debauched license to drink. *For indeed, a fellowship of life is more rightly and honorably established as the purpose of a banquet than a licentious invitation to many cups.* <note>original: "Communio siquidem vitæ rectius honestiúsq; statuitur conuiuij finis, quàm poculorum licentior inuitatio."</note> It is as if he wanted to say: although people gather to refresh themselves by eating and drinking, it is nonetheless not a fitting thing to take such license that one oversteps the bounds of reason; seeing as it is not a brotherly union, nor one that tends toward charity <note>In the 16th-century sense of *caritas*, meaning selfless Christian love for one's neighbor.</note>, to corrupt oneself or provoke others to do the same. For if you do not love yourself, how will you love your neighbor? *To whom will he be good, who is cruel to himself?* <note>original: "Qui sibi nequam, cui bonus erit?" A citation from the biblical Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 14:5.</note> It is not likely that you love yourself when you go about bringing on illness through excessive gluttony. I would also think, for my part, that those who invited me in such a manner had called or summoned me rather to keep them company in drinking, or to make me fall into dishonor or sickness, than for any other reason.

<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader," building on the previous discussion of moderation. Here, the author acknowledges the reputation for excess in antiquity but argues that Christian morality demands higher standards, while also defending the true character of wise ancients against the behavior of a debauched few. The catchword "bendum" refers to the Latin *bibendum* (to drink), which continues the Plutarch quote on the following page.</meta>

# PREFACE

<margin>Caelius Rhodiginus, Book 7, Chapter 26.</margin>
I know that, notwithstanding, the custom of the Greeks was inviolably kept, as Cicero holds, which was: *Drink, or depart.* **"Among the Greeks,"** he says, **"the law is upheld: let him either drink or leave"** <note>original: "Apud Græcos, lex obtinetur, aut bibat, aut abeat"</note>; and besides this, they would sing these verses to one another: **"Drink five, drink three, drink four cups"** <note>original: "Bibe quinque, bibe tres, bibe quatuor cyathos"</note>—meaning that each person in the company was required to drink twelve goblets. Furthermore, there is what Pliny observed of the Romans: namely, *that whoever sat at the table should not rise from it without vomiting; and to add to this, to drink competitively a full glass without breathing, leaving nothing behind, and not being permitted to cough after drinking.* But if one wished to shield oneself behind and take advantage of such a gluttonous and excessive antiquity, then I would have to compare the law of Christians with that of the pagans—those who propose for themselves an eternal bliss <term>beatitude</term> <note>From the Latin *beatitudo*, referring to the state of supreme spiritual happiness or blessedness.</note> consisting in the soul, with those who thought that once the body is dead, nothing else remains. In the meantime, I will answer those people with what the

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Love of Wealth*.</margin>
philosopher said: **"They feast as if they were to die any day"** <note>original: "Tales epulantur quasi quotidie morituri"</note>; let such people drink and eat as if they were to die from one day to the next. For nothing is more certain than what the proverb says: **"Drunkenness kills more than the sword"** <note>original: "Plures ebrietas quàm gladius necat"</note>. That is to say, that drunkenness kills more than the blade. For the rest, I do not believe that the saying of such an author concerns the general population of all the Latins, or even all the Greeks, but only some infamous or base individuals, who nevertheless cause such an epithet to be given to an entire monarchy honored by so many sages and philosophers. And to show that this is not the case,

<margin>In *The Banquet of the Seven Sages*.</margin>
Plutarch clearly holds the contrary when he speaks of the sobriety of the ancients, saying thus: **"The ancients, when they invited others to drink by offering a toast..."** <note>original: "Veteres in conuiuiis quum propinando inuitarent ad bi-"</note>

<summary>The author critiques the legendary drinking habits of the Greeks and Romans, contrasting pagan excess with Christian spiritual focus while arguing that true ancient wisdom actually favored sobriety.</summary>
<keywords>Cicero, Pliny, Plutarch, temperance, drunkenness, Christian vs. pagan morality, ancient banquets, sobriety</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader." # TO THE READER.

...to drink <note>original: "bibendum"</note>; we have heard that he used that Homeric saying:

> *As much as each is able, with certain reason and measure,*
> ->*Let him drink.*<- <note>original: "Quantum quisque potest, certa ratione modoq; Hauriat."</note>

We know, he says, that the ancients, when they invited each other to drink at their banquets, retained this Homeric verse: *Let each drink as much as he can by certain reason and measure.* The same author, being asked by the majority of the guests to accept the title of Symposiarch <term>Symposiarch</term> <note>The "master of the banquet" or toastmaster in ancient Greece, responsible for deciding how much wine would be mixed with water and how much everyone should drink.</note>, and the task of commanding the table, accepted it using such a phrase:

<margin>Decade 1, Problem 4.</margin>
*Since it seems good to you all, I appoint myself your symposiarch, and I command that everyone else may drink as they please for the time being.* <note>original: "Si quidem vobis videtur omnibus, meipsum symposiarcham vestrum creo, iubeoq;, cæteros quidem omnes pro libito in præsentia bibere."</note>

Since it seems good to you all, I declare myself your symposiarch, and for this reason I presently command each one to drink according to his will. And although such authors may serve as a defense for us; nevertheless, because we do so for a different respect than they did, we still do not stop at such proof and example. Rather, the examples we ought to use are of those who, recognizing the same God as we do, had regard not so much for health and bodily preservation through such sobriety, as for spiritual prosperity. Such was the banquet of Tobit <term>Tobit</term> <note>A righteous figure from the deuterocanonical Book of Tobit, known for his piety and adherence to Jewish law during the exile.</note> and his family, but above all the children of Job, of whom Origen <term>Origen</term> <note>An early Christian scholar and theologian (c. 184–253 AD) from Alexandria.</note> speaks:

<margin>Book 1 on Job.</margin>
*They held drinking parties, but not for drunkenness, nor for excess, nor for indecency, for intemperance or for illicit foolery, any more than for pleasant games or immodest and dirty words.*

I will add further the censure of St. John Chrysostom <term>St. John Chrysostom</term> <note>An important Early Church Father (c. 347–407 AD) known for his powerful preaching; his name "Chrysostom" means "golden-mouthed."</note> for a more ample confirmation;

<margin>Homily 57 to the people of Antioch.</margin>
*Where there is drunkenness, there is the devil; where there is satiety, there the demons lead the [dances]* <note>original: "Vbi ebrietas, ibi diabolus; vbi saturitas, illic dæmones choreas" - the catchword 'reas' completes the Latin word 'choreas'.</note>

<meta>catchword: dances [choreas]</meta>

<meta>This page continues the preface "To the Reader," moving from ancient examples of drinking customs to Christian and moral warnings against excess at the table. # PREFACE

...carry out their business. Where there is drunkenness, there the devil is; and where indecent words are spoken, or where there is satiety and gluttony, there the demons lead the dance. These grave and highly authoritative sayings will suffice to teach the uncivil how they must behave at the table without seeking further proof. First, one should not be there for drunkenness, or excess in drinking and eating. Second, not for a disordered and carnal appetite. Third, not to act the buffoon and incite others to loose living; and fourthly, one must not come to use lascivious, ill-considered, and impudent words. And I have no doubt that the banquet is more perfectly fulfilled by these latter conditions than by the former, and furthermore, the company will be more satisfied. For what profit is there in the beauty of the men, the pleasure of the place, the clear weather, or the exquisite sauces of the meats, if the men are unpleasant, either through excess, intemperance, or quarrelsome words? And especially regarding the last point concerning words; what if they are stinging to those present, throwing barbs <note>original: "brocards"</note> at one another? It is a difficult thing to digest when one hears their absent friends being attacked by the reckless tongue of someone. This is what

<margin>Aelian, Book 11.</margin>
that common slanderer Zoilus <note>Zoilus was a 4th-century BC Greek grammarian known as "the scourge of Homer" for his harsh and constant criticism.</note> replied, who, when asked why he spoke ill of everyone, answered that it was because he could not do them physical harm. And it seems that the Prophet David wished to set himself against such a man more than any other who ever lived, when he said:

<margin>Psalm 100.</margin>
*I persecuted those who slandered their neighbor in secret.* <note>This refers to Psalm 101:5 in most modern English Bibles; the author uses the Vulgate numbering (Psalm 100).</note>

<margin>At dinners.</margin>
And to resume our discourse, I say with Macrobius <term>Macrobius</term> <note>A 5th-century Roman writer whose work "Saturnalia" describes a series of learned conversations held at a banquet.</note> that one must hold conversations at a banquet as

<meta>catchword: much (tant)</meta>

It cites classical figures like Zoilus and Macrobius alongside King David to emphasize that virtuous conversation is more important to a banquet's success than the quality of the food.</summary>

->TO THE READER.<-

as much whole in chastity as appealing in good grace. <margin>Sermon 24 on Romans 13.</margin> St. John Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD) was an important Early Church Father known for his preaching; his surname means "golden-mouthed."</note> says very well: *Our speech should not be unseemly and improper, but grave and moderate; and we should be satisfied with food, not undone by pleasures.* <note>original: "Non indecēs & indecorus sermo, sed grauis & moderatus, & cibo expleri, non frangi in oblectamētis ducenda sunt."</note> One must take recreation not in unseemly or uncivil talk, but in grave and moderate talk, being content with honest nourishment and not exceeding it. <margin>In the Banquet of the Seven Sages.</margin> The wise man, says Plutarch, does not go to a banquet with the intention of filling himself up to the throat like some vessel, but to the end that he might treat matters both seriously and joyfully, listening and speaking appropriately as time and opportunity present themselves. And in the same place, the said author speaks of nothing else but the fine conversations held between these great philosophers, proposing some riddle, some recreational matter—in short, that which deserved to be examined by such learned men. <margin>Germanicus in the Chronicles.</margin> Thus Cleobulus proposed such a riddle: There is a father who has twelve children, each of whom has thirty children of different forms—namely, some white and others black—and although they are immortal, they all decay and perish. <note>This is a classic riddle of the ancient world: the father is the Year, the twelve children are the Months, and the thirty children are the Days and Nights.</note> Another proposed it in this way: Who is he who, not being born, is nonetheless dead? Or who is he who, being born, has escaped death? Or who is he who, being dead, was not infected by corruption? Another was presented to the wise Bias <note>Bias of Priene was one of the Seven Sages of Greece, known for his legal and verbal wit.</note> by the King of Ethiopia, having received it from Amasis, King of Egypt, which was to drink the entire sea; to which he responded on the spot. <note>Bias famously replied that he would drink the sea if the King first stopped all the rivers from flowing into it, as he had only agreed to drink the sea water, not the river water.</note> But besides this, the answers given promptly and unexpectedly to a question from the same seemed to show an even greater liveliness of spirit, such as that of Hesiod to the question of Homer, saying:

B iiij

# P R E F A C E

->*O Muse, tell me of things that will never be, nor ever were!*<-
<note>original: "Quae nec erũt, mihi Muſa refer, nunquãve fuere!" This line is often associated with the legendary poetic contests of antiquity.</note>
to which Hesiod <note>Hesiod was an ancient Greek poet active around the same time as Homer (8th century BC).</note> responded:
->*When the fierce steed, with a powerful blow, shall have shattered*<-
->*the wagon at Jupiter’s tomb, and depart having gained the palm of victory.*<-
<note>original: "Quum Iovis ad tumulũ valido perfregerit ictu / Plauſtra ferox ſonipes, palmaq; potitus abibit."</note>

And just as it was with the pagans, so in the time of the early Church, those good figures, filled with spiritual graces, dealt with nothing else but holy and spiritual practice, being attentive to it with a resonance of divine songs. From this, I believe, some trace of such a praiseworthy custom still remains among us; for according to the quality of the persons and their vocation, they have discussed, and continue to discuss, subjects suitable to the company to which they are invited. For instance: among the pious, they speak of devout things; among poets, of fictions; among philosophers, of natural things; and among those who have more experience in Holy Scripture, they speak sometimes of the dignity and excellence of man, sometimes of the mutation and changeability of things, sometimes of the ingratitude of man toward his Lord, and sometimes of the misfortunes that overwhelm him—so that he might not wrongly cry out and say what those who call themselves the enemies of fortune say:

->*As wave follows wave, and one day follows another, so*<-
->*To his woes my woes follow; my accumulated sorrows*<-
->*Ever accompany those that have passed.*<-

And so, remembering such banquets, let us consider that although these are established for recreation, they must nevertheless be conducted without dissolution, without slander or scandal; so that we may say with the Royal Prophet David:

<margin>Psalm 33.</margin>
<note>In modern Bibles, this is usually cited as Psalm 34:1.</note>
->*The praise of God is continually in my mouth.*<-

Following this, our discussion being prompted by the times...

# TO THE READER.

& misfortunes <note>Continuing from the previous page: "the misfortunes that overwhelm [man]..."</note> of the same, both in general and in particular, led us by a digression to speak of those sorcerers; who exercise such great rage and cruelty, appearing transformed into wild beasts, and dare to attack all sorts of people, both great and small, to devour them; where, as we continued to share examples that each had seen on every side, it was finally affirmatively proposed by a certain honorable personage that what appears outwardly to be a wolf is indeed one in truth and in fact; this he learnedly proved through reasons, examples, and authorities. 

But because our time was brief, and the Theological dispute <term>dispute Theologique: a formal academic or clerical debate regarding religious doctrine</term> complex, it seemed best to me to pause and go no further without first consulting the Fathers <note>Refers to the "Church Fathers," the influential early Christian theologians whose writings established orthodoxy.</note>, for fear of advancing some claim rashly. Having then arranged the opinions of one and all in order, it seemed good to the members of our company to bring this work into the open and before the sight of all, to serve as a resolution for others who might doubt such things in the future. 

Do not think, therefore (O friend Reader), that I was moved to do this out of ostentation or anything of the kind, but rather to satisfy that honorable company; for this reason—and because not everyone was proficient in Latin—I have set it down in the common tongue <note>original: "langue vulgaire"; the author is justifying the use of French over Latin to ensure the text is accessible to a non-scholarly audience.</note>. If it pleases you to use it, accept the good will of the author; if his capacity extended further and to higher matters, he would be ready to apply himself to them.

->To God.<-

->ELEIDON.<-

I begin to recognize the subject of your silence, and I confirm what you say; nonetheless, it seems to me that in your enumeration <note>Referring to Scipion’s list of natural and man-made disasters.</note> some point is missing. For we know it is a natural thing for the air to perform its operations; and it is a naturalized accident for man to rise up against his fellow, which was practiced by the first-born among men <note>A biblical reference to Cain, who committed the first murder by killing his brother Abel.</note>. Moreover, that the world experiences such vicissitude and change is notorious enough to us (and would to God it were not so much). In short, all these threats have been described to us in the Gospel. But although they are marvelous and strange, there are nonetheless others of even greater wonder <note>original: "admiration." In this period, "admiration" meant a state of being struck by wonder or astonishment, often at something supernatural or terrifying.</note>.

### PROTERON.

Is it possible that even more terrifying things are yet to happen? Is there anything more horrifying than to hear tell of hellish torments <note>original: "gehennes" — referring to Gehenna, a place of suffering, used here to mean both spiritual hell and physical torture</note>, tortures, prisons, ransoms, forced contributions <term>contributions: refers to "contributions of war," which were essentially legalized extortion where armies demanded money from local populations to avoid being pillaged</term>, pillaging, assassinations, and such other cruelties? To see one province against another; city against city; the father against the son; the son against his mother? What can be more deplorable than to have the clamor of the poor, exiled and banished, continually in our ears?

<margin>Ronsard</margin>
<note>These lines are from Pierre de Ronsard’s "Discours des misères de ce temps" (1562), a poem reflecting on the chaos of the French Wars of Religion.</note>
> *And to see, step by step, the desolate mothers,*
> *With their little children tenderly embraced,*
> *Going from door to door to beg for their lives,*
> *From those who, just a short while before, were the ones to ask [favors].*

And sometimes, out of despair (oh, excessive pity!), to forsake and renounce our God. So it is that those who think they have escaped the hands of God through plague or famine have not escaped Him through war. For if we have been threatened with these, it was to make us recognize the severity of such calamities.

### PROTERON.

Even if you add the irrational beasts, I find it no more strange than the previous points; since we find through many examples that beasts have in the past waged war against man just as much as man himself has risen up against his fellow. The histories bear witness to this; Holy Scripture is full of it, and consequently our experience is better assured of it: so much so that if one sees a province or region attacked—one by serpents, another by birds of prey, this one by monsters, that one by wild and furious beasts—one must not marvel at it, seeing as it is not only now that they begin [their attacks]. A very short time after the creation of man, this other punishment was added: that the creatures by whom, through divine ordinance, man should have been recognized <note>meaning recognized as their master or sovereign</note>, are those by whom he is primarily agitated and tormented. And in truth, it is one of the first jurisdictions that God had granted him when He directed His word toward him and his wife:

<margin>Genesis 1</margin>

# ON LYCANTHROPY

...the hundred and third <note>This refers to Pope Innocent III (reigned 1198–1216), who wrote a famous and gloomy treatise on the misery of human life.</note> has made an enumeration of them, in which he does not only include the enemies

...to approach, it would not have been without the danger of his life. From this, I infer that if God does not look upon His people with the eye of His mercy, then such punishments by beasts, or otherwise, are but the beginning of sorrows for the vicious and enormous excesses that we commit from day to day.

### ELEION.

I know well that we have deserved the wrath of our God, for which reason every creature is rightly ready—avenging the injury done to its Creator—to rise up against man, and especially those beasts of which you have recited a great number. But alas! Those of which I mean to speak are not such as those, nor are they comprised of any species of monstrous serpent or similar creature; rather, it is the human species which, through some unknown change, renders itself like the irrational and brute beasts, and in such a likeness commits things contrary to its nature.

# OF LYCANTHROPY.

<margin>*Aristot. book 1 Politics*</margin>
*Just as man is the best of all animals when he enjoys the law: so is he the worst of all animals when separated from law and justice.* <note>original: "Sicut omnium animalium melior est homo lege fruens: Sic omnium animalium pessimum, à lege & iustitia separatus."</note> Just as among the animals there is none better than a man living according to right and reason: so is there none worse than the same man when he withdraws himself from the path of equity and justice. # ON LYCANTHROPY.

fox; the Pharisees were of the race of Abraham, and not literally a brood of vipers; but we say this only according to the **effects**.

### ELEION.

What do you mean by the **effects**?

### PROTERON.

The **effects** are that, just as the fox is cunning <term>cauteleuse: Derived from the Latin "cautela," this term describes a deceitful, cautious, or crafty malice.</term>, and vipers are harmful to their own parents, dogs are shameless, swine are lustful, and so on with the others; in the same way Herod was a fox because of his cunning malice and shrewdness; the Pharisees were a "brood of vipers," plotting against their own blood (who were the prophets). In short, they were all performing a **beastly function** rather than a human one, looking toward the earth and not toward heaven; for the contemplation of which they were created and placed in this world; ungrateful and unmindful of the special and particular graces that God had bestowed upon them. It does not seem out of place to introduce a few verses on this subject from that noble French poet <note>Pierre de Ronsard (1524–1585), the leading figure of the French Renaissance poetic group known as La Pléiade.</note>, where he reproaches men for provoking our God with their vices to become angry and to unleash upon them the terrifying lightning bolt of his wrath:

<margin>Ronsard.</margin>
> *Man alone, man whom you have made*
> *More perfect than any other animal.*
> *In whom you placed the features of your image,*
> *And toward the sky you raised his face.*
> *To whom you granted so many graces to possess,*
> *In whom you placed judgment and knowledge.*

Bede <note>Bede the Venerable (c. 673–735 AD), an English monk and scholar known as the "Father of English History."</note> compares such kinds of people, as well as the persecutors of the Church, to the lion and the bear attacking the sheep of David. For Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca the Younger (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> seemed to experience this in his time, when with grave exaggeration and horror he spoke these words:

<margin>Seneca, Book 1 of *On Mercy*. <note>original: "Seneca. li. 10. de cle." The text likely contains a printer's error, as Seneca's *De Clementia* consists of only two books.</note></margin>

This rage, he says, may well be called beastly: to rejoice and take pleasure in blood and wounds, and having rejected the state of man, to change into a wild beast.

->SCIPION<-

Can men, then, leave their natural human form to clothe themselves in another?

->PROTERON<-

There is no one who can maintain that such a thing is possible.

->ELEION<-

Do you not believe in transformation at all—namely, that a man can take another bodily form, or be transmuted into a figure other than what he is?

->PROTERON<-

It could not be done except by divine power, and I do not believe otherwise.

# DIALOGUE
## SCIPION

Nevertheless, you have already put forward an authority from Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca the Younger (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher and tutor to Nero.</note>, by which it seems that in his time there were men who, delighting in human blood, changed their human form into the likeness of savage animals.

## PROTERON

By that, I did not mean that he [the man] abandoned his human form, but rather that men could be found who were so cruel that they deserved to be called brute beasts <note>In Renaissance thought, "brute beasts" lacked the "rational soul" that defined humanity.</note> rather than rational creatures, because they took delight in every kind of impiety. Furthermore, if we must go deeper, we might say that Seneca could have experienced such cruelty and barbarism in that abominable and detestable tyrant Nero <note>Nero (37–68 AD) was the Roman Emperor notorious for his perceived tyranny and the first Roman persecution of Christians.</note>. Not content with persecuting and afflicting the Christians with a thousand deaths and tortures, Nero even dared (a thing horrible to hear) to dress himself in the freshly flayed <note>original: "escorchée"</note> skin of a wild beast, so that under this cover he might go and eat the thighs of the crucified while they were still living. This, in my opinion, is what his teacher Seneca was aiming at—not to say that there was a man who changed into another physical form, but rather that this tyrant disguised himself to hide his wickedness. Moreover, one can easily be deceived by such opinions, especially as it seems to me you may have heard mention of so many kinds of monsters resembling the human species, which might cause you to fall into error. I will try to resolve this for you in a few pa- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "de pa-," likely leading into "de paroles" (of words) or "de passages."</note>

<summary>Proteron argues that lycanthropy is often a metaphor for extreme human cruelty, citing the Emperor Nero’s horrific habit of disguising himself in animal skins to commit atrocities as the true meaning behind Seneca’s descriptions of men becoming beasts.</summary>

present in the land of Galicia, in the Spanish Isles <note>likely referring to the Canary Islands or the Balearic Islands, which were often points of curiosity in early modern geography</note>, men who live as if they were wild beasts, fleeing before the inhabitants; and of whom a male and a female were captured in the country of Saxony by hunters, and they possessed entirely human form, so says Albert <note>Albertus Magnus, the medieval scholar mentioned on the previous page</note>. ### S C I P I O N.

We have so many examples of it, that it seems impossible to me to say the contrary: namely, of men who, by turning and converting themselves into a foreign form, devour the people they encounter, and even other animals, and above all young children. And as for me, I do not say that these are monsters, but truly men, and naturally of the same species as you and I.

# OF LYCANTHROPY.

# DIALOGUE

This accords with and suits the Platonic or Pythagorean doctrine; for all these thinkers believe this transformation to be real: namely, that he who has lived by robbery and in lust shall change into a hawk; <note>original: "milan" — a bird of prey often used as a symbol of greed</note> he who has fought valiantly shall be converted into a lion; into a dragon, he who sets himself against the human race; and into a man shall he be changed who has lived a civil life. This doctrine, though followed in the past, was held by very few—such as Ovid with his *Metamorphoses*, or Pythagoras with his oft-repeated metempsychosis <note>the Greek term for the soul's transfer into a new body after death</note>—namely, as Saint Jerome <note>a prominent 4th-century scholar and theologian known for translating the Bible into Latin</note> says, he who first remembered having been Euphorbus, afterwards Callides, then Hermotimus, after having been Pyrrhus, and finally Pythagoras. Saint Jerome mocks this with many arguments. But who would not say it is an illusion, and worthy of mockery, to follow such an opinion: that the rational, voluntary, intellectual, and discursive soul should pass into the body of a goldfinch, a cat, a rooster, a dog, or a swan; and the soul of the swan, in turn, organize a human body?

<meta>This page continues the critique of Platonic and Pythagorean transmigration of souls, transitioning from historical mockery to a philosophical and theological refutation based on the different natures of the soul and the arguments of the Church Fathers.</meta>

# ON LYCANTHROPY <note>A term derived from the Greek *lykos* (wolf) and *anthropos* (human), referring here to the broader concept of humans transforming into animals.</note>

...did not speculate upon or consider nature as much as Aristotle did afterward. If they had done so, they would have perceived that the **brutal soul** <note>original: "ame brutale" — in classical philosophy, the soul shared with animals, responsible for movement and sensation but lacking reason</note> cannot reason, the **vegetative soul** <note>original: "ame vegetatiue" — the soul shared with plants, responsible for growth and nutrition</note> has no feeling, and the **rational soul** <note>original: "ame raisonnable" — the uniquely human soul capable of logic and morality</note> differs entirely from those two. Leaving aside this opinion then, and understanding the one we must hold, let us first show that the judgment of those thinkers is false.

<margin>Justin Martyr, in *Questions to the Christians*, prop.</margin>
Justin Martyr <note>a 2nd-century Christian philosopher and apologist</note> confounds Plato and his followers with their own weapon <note>original: "de leur propre cousteau" — literally "with their own knife," meaning using their own logic against them</note>. Through this opinion and another equally erroneous one, he leads them to a very dangerous absurdity, proving by their own opinions that if man changes, then consequently God must change. For he says: If it is as it seems to the heathens—namely, that God did not create or make man by His own will, but that the being of God alone necessarily implies the existence of man—it follows that the being of God depends on the being of man. Consequently, if man changes or is **transmuted** from his human figure and form into an ant or another animal, then God must necessarily be changed and transmuted from His nature into another. Thus they are confounded by their own reasoning. And once again, the same author opposes this same opinion in his *Dialogue with Trypho* against the Jews.

<margin>Augustine, *The Literal Meaning of Genesis*, Book 7, Chapter 11.</margin>
And as for Saint Augustine, he holds such a fantasy to be a **diabolical illusion**;

> Those things, he says, which are reported to have happened—such as people seemingly remembering that they were in the bodies of certain animals—either these things are told falsely, <note>original Latin: "Illa, quæ feruntur accidisse; ut quidam quasi recordarentur, quod in quorundam animalium corporibus fuerint; aut falsa narrantur"</note>

D iij
<meta>catchword: or lu-</meta>

<summary>The author uses Aristotelian categories of the soul and the writings of Justin Martyr and Saint Augustine to argue that the physical transformation of humans into animals is a philosophical impossibility and a diabolical deception.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Justin Martyr, Saint Augustine, types of souls, transmigration, diabolical illusion, transmutation, theology vs philosophy</keywords>

# DIALOGUE

*...or this was done in their minds by the deceptions of demons.* <note>original Latin: "aut ludificationibus dæmonum hoc in eorum animis factũ est"</note> What is recounted as having happened—that some people remembered having been in the bodies of animals—is either told as a falsehood, or else it occurred in their minds through diabolical illusions. And the same is said in mockery of the **recollection** <term>reminiscence: the Platonic theory that "learning" is actually the soul recovering knowledge it possessed before being born into a body</term> of Plato, and the **metempsychosis** <term>metempsychosis: the transmigration of a soul into a new body, whether human or animal</term> of Pythagoras;

<margin>Augustine, Book 11 of *The Trinity*, chapter 15.</margin>
The aforementioned theory of recollection takes its starting point from the fact that Plato, seeing a young child answer geometry questions appropriately, took the opportunity to imagine that souls must have previously existed in this world. In his view, by learning, one remembers what was already known, rather than learning something new. But the holy father [Augustine] responds to him in two ways: First, that this was the result of a sound and natural judgment in his responses, and that others could have just as easily answered such questions, even though there had been very few geometers before then. Second, that if it were so, the rest of mankind would remember what happened to them in their other bodies just as well as that boy did; and yet, there were very few who remembered anything. For this reason, the holy Doctor, fearing that the faithful might embrace such an absurdity, provides us with this resolution, saying:

<margin>Augustine, Book 7 of *The Literal Meaning of Genesis*, chapter 9.</margin>
*We must beware of believing that any transfer of the soul from a beast to a man can occur, for this is entirely contrary to the truth and the Catholic faith.* <note>original Latin: "Cauendũ est ne quadā trāslatio animæ fieri à pecore in hominẽ posse credatur, quod veritati fideiq; Catholicæ omnino contrariũ est."</note> It is necessary that we guard ourselves against believing that any transfer of the soul can take place from the body of a brute animal into a man: for that is...

<summary>This page uses the writings of Saint Augustine to refute the Platonic idea that the soul "remembers" past lives and the Pythagorean idea that souls move between species. Augustine argues that "learning" is natural reason at work, not a memory of a previous existence in another body.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Augustine, Plato, Pythagoras, metempsychosis, recollection, transmigration of souls, geometry, diabolical illusions, soul, Catholic faith</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, shifting from Saint Augustine's critiques of Plato and Pythagoras to the views of the early Church father Origen. ### ON LYCANTHROPY.

...entirely contrary to the truth and to the Catholic faith.

And furthermore:
> In whatever way the opinion of Philosophers regarding the revolutions of souls may or may not be held, it is nonetheless not fitting for the Catholic faith to believe that the souls of beasts migrate into humans, or those of humans into beasts. <note>original Latin: "Quoquo modo se habeat vel nō habeat opinio Philosophorū de reuolutionibus animarum, Catholica tamen fidei non cōuenit credere, animas pecorū in homines, aut hominū in pecora transmigrare."</note>

In whatever manner the Philosophers have opined regarding the revolution of souls, it is nevertheless not fitting for the Catholic faith to believe that the souls of beasts pass and change into the bodies of men, and reciprocally the souls of men into the bodies of beasts. And although this ancient Doctor **Origen** <note>Origen (c. 184–253 AD) was an early Christian scholar and theologian whose views on the pre-existence of souls were later considered controversial.</note> is sometimes accused of having favored such an imagination, he nevertheless contradicts it, as can be seen in this passage:

<margin>Origen, Treatise 3 on Matthew, chapter 17.</margin>
*Elijah has already come*, where he says thus:
> Regarding what is said because of John, *Elijah has already come*, one must not understand it as the soul of Elijah, lest we fall into the dogma of transcorporation; which is foreign to ecclesiastical truth. <note>original Latin: "Quod dicit propter Ioannē, Helias iā venit, non anima Helia intelligenda est, ne incidamus in dogma trāscorporationis; quod alienū est ab ecclesiastica veritate."</note>

What our Lord said speaking of Saint John—that Elijah is already come—must not be understood as the soul of Elijah, for fear that we might come to fall into the opinion of **transcorporation** <term>transcorporation: an early term for reincarnation, the movement of a soul into a new physical body</term>, which is entirely foreign to ecclesiastical truth. In the same place, this Doctor shows how this would lead to the absurdity that the world would never end; this would happen if it were necessary to change bodies every time one committed an offense. Such a concept is entirely against the holy scriptures and the word of Jesus Christ himself. And how many years, he asks, do you think the soul could remain blameless and clean of sin within the body?

From which I further infer that they could not introduce or maintain any true memory. For if one remembers and recalls, then there is no forgetting; and if there has been forgetting, the memory must be false. The conclusion, therefore, is that souls never make such a change because of some sin or malice, and that God has other prisons to put them in than within bodies. Furthermore, this is against our faith, as the Holy Doctors <note>The "Holy Doctors" refers to influential Christian theologians, such as St. Augustine or St. Thomas Aquinas, whose works defined Church doctrine.</note> testify to us. Moreover, a very certain axiom of all philosophers is that every <term>substantial form</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "form" is the essential nature or "blueprint" of a thing that makes it what it is (e.g., a human soul is the form of a human body).</note> requires certain dispositions to inform the matter it claims, and all the more so as the perfection of the said form is more sovereign. If this is so, how much should we think that the soul—which is a form so noble and so perfect that there is none better in the world—will require greater and more exquisite dispositions to be able to inform a body so different, such as a wolf or another brute beast? You fall into great difficulties.

For we can do no less than judge by what we see and handle, according to the maxim of Aristotle: *He who perceives, judges* <note>original Latin: "Qui sentit indicat"</note>. That is to say, we judge by means of the senses.

-> THE FIRST PROPOSITION <- <note>original Greek: "Proteron," meaning the first or prior point in an argument.</note>

Supposing then that a transmutation or transformation takes place, it must be either natural, voluntary, or violent (since all transformation is included among the categories of movement). That it could be natural is impossible, for there would be a manifest repugnance in nature, seeing as nature cannot be changed except by its own principle, which is God, <margin>Thomas Aquinas, *Summa Theologica*, Part 3, Question 13, Article 2.</margin> (as St. Thomas says), to whom alone it belongs to transmute creatures (either mediately or immediately), seeing as to Him alone belongs the power to preserve them. Therefore, it cannot be natural, for we find no record that God ever made or used such metamorphoses or transformations, any more than...

# ON LYCANTHROPY #

...ural, [I] remember that in the year 1587, being in Périgord, and staying for several months in a small convent and the town of Rioms <note>Likely modern-day Rions, situated on the Garonne.</note>, specifically five leagues <term>league: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 2.5 miles or 4 kilometers.</term> from Bordeaux, I was sent on St. John’s Day <note>June 24th, the Feast of St. John the Baptist, a major midsummer celebration.</note> by the Father Guardian of the said place to a certain village about three leagues distant. Returning from there and passing through another small village, I noticed a poor woman, deeply distressed, who warned me against taking that same path; she said this because not even half an hour had passed since a little girl had been snatched from her doorstep by a wolf. Despite whatever shouts or cries the woman and several of her neighbors could raise, the child could never be recovered. This is the first instance I heard spoken of, but not the last.

Moving from there to Toulouse for my course of studies, the aforementioned Father Guardian wrote to the head of our convent—the late Reverend Father de Roca, of blessed memory—letters of recommendation asking for the prayers of the Religious <note>Refers to the monks or friars of the order.</note> regarding such poverty and misery: namely, how several men, women, and little children had gone missing without any news, having been devoured by wolves or other wild beasts. As evidence of this, when it came time to reap the grain, people found hidden skulls <note>original: "tez", an archaic form of *têtes*.</note> and bones of arms and legs. Nor is that all. For little by little in this same country, and even more so among their neighbors the Gascons and other surrounding lands, these wolves became so bold that from then on, no one dared to go through the fields without...

...company. Three or four years later, having completed my said course <note>Referring to his university or theological studies in Toulouse mentioned on the previous page.</note>, and beginning to return toward our quarters, I was assigned by my office to Rodez, the capital city of Rouergue and bordering Auvergne, where similarly these wolves roamed in large packs <note>original: "loups a grandes touppes." While *touppe* can mean a tuft of hair or a thicket, here it refers to the wolves appearing in dense groups or perhaps having thick, winter coats.</note>, so much so that around ten o’clock in the evening, while studying in our small cell, I heard a noise and howling of such harmony <note>The narrator uses "harmony" ironically to describe the coordinated, eerie sound of the pack.</note> that I had never heard the like.

Curious to know what it could be, after having finished my discourse <note>Likely his evening prayers, a lesson, or a written composition.</note>, I went quickly to inquire about this new and unheard-of harmony from the convent's watchman, whom I found still terrified with fear; he told me that he had never seen such a company, nor wolves so enraged. There were eighteen or twenty of them, as he had been able to notice by the light of the moon. Moreover, they were so bold that they seemed to defy all the inhabitants of the town, approaching even the moats <note>original: "fossez." These were the defensive ditches surrounding the fortified walls of Rodez.</note>. And as for their boldness, I can say what happened to me. In this same region near Villefranche <note>Likely Villefranche-de-Rouergue.</note>, I encountered one very close to me on a narrow path, who would hardly turn aside despite whatever shouting I could manage; at last, however, it went away slowly, as God willed.

...with a three-pronged pitchfork arranged in a triangle <note>original: "fourchefiere a trois pointes en triangle." A defensive weapon similar to a pitchfork, often used by peasants to keep predatory animals at a distance.</note>, for it is the staff that has been found most suitable for resisting the rage of such beasts; and in this manner, a great number of them have already been dispatched. The common people are of the opinion that hunger—pressing these animals due to the scarcity of livestock, which has almost all perished because of the war <note>Likely referring to the French Wars of Religion, which devastated the countryside and its economy in the late 16th century.</note>—has driven them into this frenzy to turn upon men. Those I heard howling, which was around Saint Martin’s Day <note>November 11th.</note>, were said to have descended from the mountains of Auvergne, as they did every year, to find food. This was plausible, for some months before All Saints’ Day <note>November 1st.</note>, the shepherds, no longer able to remain in these mountains because of the excessive cold, returned to their homes with their flocks; and thus, you see how wolves, being pressed by hunger, can naturally attack men. However, I shall go even further and say that this does not always happen because of their appetite or their own impulse, but sometimes while being driven by an evil spirit.

<margin>Part I, Question 10.</margin> *The Hammer of Witches* <note>original: *Malleus Malefic.* A famous 15th-century treatise on the detection and prosecution of witches.</note> cites the opinion of Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a philosopher and theologian who wrote extensively on natural history.</note> in his book *On Animals* <note>original: *de animalib.*</note>, saying that these incursions of wolves occur for several reasons: sometimes out of hunger, at other times because of their great strength, occasionally when they have young, and sometimes also due to old age; for as they can no longer hunt or run after other animals, they then become more dangerous to men. They also turn more readily toward [experience]... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "l'expe-", with the catchword "rience" indicating the word "expérience" (experience) begins the next page.</note>
