the children; and as it was that the evil spirit only truly possessed a wolf, this poor man thought himself to be such a wolf, and carried out his deeds devouring the children, and was for a long time mocked for such a frenzy <note>In this context, frenzy refers to a state of madness or delirium (phrenesie).</note>, until he was found lying in the middle of a forest, where he was entranced. Now, that he takes the human body is notorious enough, not only for those who are possessed (of whom some are still seen in this time) for their demerit or testing, but also others of whom I will provide only one example:

<margin>Book 3 of *The Demonomania*, chapter 3.</margin>

Bodin <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), an influential French jurist and demonologist who wrote *De la démonomanie des sorciers*.</note> relates in the name of Manilius that three personages, richly dressed, were asking for the daughter of a certain townsman (who also invited them to dinner, along with a learned Theologian to keep them company). But since these aforementioned suitors took no pleasure in the fine words of this good man, and the host said to them: "Depart from here, you despisers of God," immediately three dead bodies of hanged men fell to the ground from nearby, which stank strangely.

<margin>SCIPION.</margin>

However, the examples we find and perceive do not allow us to believe that these are only wolves, diabolical agitations, or bodies taken by demons; rather, they are certainly Lycanthropes and transformed beings, as we know by their confession <note>original: *descharge*. In 16th-century legal contexts, this refers to the testimony or deposition given by the accused.</note>;

<margin>Part 2, question 1, chapter 2.</margin>
The *Malleus Maleficarum* <note>original: "Mal. Malef."; The "Hammer of Witches," a famous 15th-century Latin treatise on the prosecution of witches.</note> also recites a great number of similar cases, but among others, that of a witch whose daughter confessed one day that she had inadvertently discovered a pot full of the heads of small children. This is horrible to hear; nevertheless, it is to show us that such barbarity has not only just now begun to be practiced. But so as not to be recognized (as they might be when going to houses at night or to another place to commit some wickedness), they transform themselves and appear in such a likeness, leaving behind their human form through diabolical art.

from which three of the leading citizen-women of the city fell into their beds. When these women were asked the cause of their so sudden and excessive illness, they accused the poor man. In truth, he was like to suffer greatly from imprisonment and other tortures, until—knowing both who his accusers were and the cause—he remembered that on the day in question, he had not touched nor struck any woman. Indeed, he protested and swore he had never even thought of it, but had rather defended himself quite well against three cats. In the end, the truth was discovered to be exactly so. How then have you doubted this until now?

Now, as for us, we maintain that such lycanthropy <note>The supernatural transformation of a person into a wolf.</note> occurred in times past; and just as it could happen then, whether by ointments, or by crossing a certain river, or otherwise, so it happens now by similar means, or by other things possessing this diabolical power and artifice. Take, for example, certain belts; when people are girded with them, they are instantly transformed into wolves, and remain so for a certain space of time. 

Such an event occurred in recent memory in a certain small town not far from Cologne called Bedburg <note>original: "Bebur". This refers to the 1589 case of Peter Stumpp, the "Werewolf of Bedburg," one of the most famous werewolf trials in history.</note>, where a certain sorcerer was executed with an extraordinary death and punishment for his excessive wickedness. He confessed to having received such a belt from the devil, which had such power that it could transform him into a werewolf <note>original: "lycanthropier"</note> whenever he pleased.

-> SCIPION. <-

It seems we can no longer doubt it.

->P R O T E R O N.<-

Inasmuch as the principal authors of whom you wish to speak are understood differently; others are suspect, and the rest are pagans. For in the first place, St. Augustine <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential Christian theologian who discussed the limits of demonic power in *The City of God*.</note> does not recite this as a thing that one ought to believe, but on the contrary, to reject it and show what he truly believes regarding it. Pliny does the same when referring to the opinion of Emanthes. Lucian, Apuleius, Virgil, and Apollonius of Tyana <note>Apollonius (c. 15–100 AD) was a philosopher and reputed miracle-worker often compared to Christ or accused of magic by early Christian writers.</note> are, and have always been, suspect to us for their sorcery, and even their atheism. Ovid, Homer, and others like them were devoted to their poetic fictions, feigning and imagining what never was, and what is impossible to be. Therefore, we do not grant them faith. Indeed, even if we wished to believe the aforementioned Ovid, we would rather stop at his retraction and final sentence than at his first, which is as follows when speaking to Augustus <note>The Roman Emperor Augustus, who exiled Ovid.</note>:

->PROTERON.<-

Bodin may well say other things (regarding this subject) that are no more true than those, based on the fiction of those ancient poets—either pagans, or others who are openly against him. For first of all, St. Thomas does not say a single word in his favor in the place cited above; rather, he responds evidently against Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037) was a Persian philosopher whose Neoplatonic views on the mind’s power to affect matter were often critiqued by Scholastic theologians.</note>, who wished to maintain (just as Bodin does) that corporeal nature obeys demons. I will add the terms which he uses for the refutation of such a proposition: *This position is contradicted by both philosophers and theologians. By philosophers indeed, because they say that the motion of the heavens is the instrument of the moving intelligence, whereby no effect can come to lower things from the intelligence except through its mediation. It is also contradicted by theologians, because corporeal forms do not come from the influence of demons, but from the influence of God, who can lead them from the potentiality of matter into act without the help of any lower agent.* <note>original: "Huic positioni contradicitur, & à philosophis & à Theologis. A philosophis quidem, quia dicunt quod motus cæli est instrumentum intelligentiæ mouentis, vnde non nisi eo mediante potest prouenire effectus in inferioribus ab intelligentia. ATheologis etiam contradicitur, quia formæ corporales non sunt ex influentia dæmonum, sed ex influentia Dei, qui eas ex potentia materiæ possit educere in actū sine adminiculo alicuius inferioris agētis."</note> He says thus: This proposition is contradicted according to the opinion of both philosophers and theologians. The philosophers say that the movement of the heavens is an instrument of the moving intelligence <note>In medieval cosmology, "intelligences" (often identified as angels) were believed to move the celestial spheres, which in turn governed physical changes on Earth.</note>, by reason of which the effect of said intelligence cannot reach lower things except by its means. He says even more: namely, that demons cannot, by their own strength and virtue, give any form immediately to matter—whether that form be accidental or substantial—but can only do so by applying **active things to passive things** <note>original: "activa passivis." A standard Scholastic principle: demons can’t "create" effects but can speed up natural processes by bringing the right ingredients together.</note> (as the **Scholastics** <term>Scholastics: medieval theologians and philosophers who used a highly structured logic to reconcile Christian doctrine with Aristotelian philosophy.</term> say). 

This means (as he gives the analogy) that just as dry wood, when brought near a fire, cannot help but burn, or just as a craftsman and laborer acts and works when he finds the matter suitable to his art so that he may easily labor and fashion it—otherwise he can do nothing—so demons can make use of **secondary causes** <note>In this worldview, God is the "Primary Cause" of everything. "Secondary causes" are the natural laws or physical interactions we see on Earth.</note>, adapting them to their wickedness. These might be imperfect creatures—namely, the lesser animals—which can be produced from the **slime of the earth** <note>This refers to the historical theory of "spontaneous generation," the belief that small creatures like vermin were born directly from mud and heat rather than from parents.</note>, occurring only through the effect of one of the primary causes, which is the Sun (without excluding the sovereign cause, which is God). Such creatures might be frogs, rats, snakes, and other such vermine, for whose generation the conjunction of male and female is not always necessary; and only these, and no others, can demons draw forth and form from the earth. 

So you see, regarding the first opinion, that this sentence cited by Bodin <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596) was a famous jurist who argued that witches and demons could physically transform people.</note> contradicts that of St. Thomas. And as for the other

is found in Case 26, Question 5, in the chapter titled *Episcopi* <note>The *Canon Episcopi* is a 10th-century legal decree (later part of the *Decretum Gratiani*) that became a foundational text for skeptics; it argued that the "night-flights" and transformations associated with witchcraft were mental delusions rather than physical realities.</note>, which speaks thus: "Whoever believes that any creature can be made, or changed for better or for worse, or transformed into another species or another likeness, except by the Creator himself who made all things, and through whom all things were made, is without doubt an infidel and worse than a pagan." <note>original: "*Quisquis credit posse fieri aliquam creaturam, aut in melius, aut in deterius commutari, aut transformari in aliam speciem, vel in aliam similitudinem, nisi ab ipso creatore qui omnia fecit, & per quem facta sunt omnia, proculdubio infidelis est & pagano deterior.*"</note> Whoever believes any creature can be made better or worse by being changed into another species, or another likeness, except by the same Creator who made all things, without doubt he is an infidel, and worse than a pagan. This is the decree, ordinance, and agreement practiced and admitted in the Church by the Fathers, who have always debated this cause as just, and worthy of contention against those who would wish to maintain the opposite—I know not what sort of people who have revolted and banded together against it. Furthermore, it would be a very heavy heresy, indeed a blasphemy, to attribute to human or diabolical forces that which belongs to God alone: namely, the creation or mutation of a wolf's body for a <term>lycanthrope</term> <note>From the Greek *lykos* (wolf) and *anthropos* (man); here referring to the physical transformation of a human into a wolf.</note> of I-know-not-what human fashion. ...a branch or broom, which the witches <note>The original French uses "sorcières," specifically female witches.</note> of Verberie, Loigny, etc., and others used, who, having placed it between their legs while saying certain words, were suddenly transported into the air? One certainly sees that the Italian Baptiste <note>Giovanni Battista della Porta (1535–1615), an Italian polymath whose book *Magia Naturalis* explored the boundaries between science and the occult.</note> in his book on magic (that is to say, witchcraft) strives to suggest that these ointments <term>onguens</term> <note>These "flying ointments" were supposedly herbal salves that allowed witches to fly or experience visions.</note> currently under discussion have some natural and soporific <note>Sleep-inducing.</note> power to cause sleep; almost persuading one to put it to the proof and experiment. If you tell me that it is by the spoken words that such charms are performed, I ask: who gave them this power? Not the devil, as we have already proven above—namely, that he can do nothing except artificial things or the representation of real ones. It would then have to come from God, and consequently be like a miracle; but we cannot say this, for it is found nowhere in Scripture that He ever granted so much power to a creature; on the contrary, He says, *My glory...* <note>original Latin: "Gloriam." This refers to Isaiah 42:8: "I am the Lord... my glory I will not give to another."</note>

Let it now be resolved that lycanthropy or transformation has no place among Catholics.

# ON LYCANTHROPY.

...done by divine power, but not by diabolical or magical virtue, such as the first examples you proposed above. Nevertheless, the examples you have now produced can generate (as they have already done) several doubts and scruples. Regarding **Nebuchadnezzar** <note>The Babylonian king described in the Book of Daniel who was humbled by God and lived like an animal for seven years.</note>, some have estimated that he was changed in appearance only and not truly; others have held it for certain that he took another form; but the latter prove neither one nor the other, and nonetheless hold the true meaning. 

For to answer the first, if he had the appearance only on the outside, and not on the inside and regarding himself, it would follow that those who saw him thus would have been punished rather than Nebuchadnezzar himself, since he would not have felt it as much as those who saw him. To say also with the others that he was truly transformed into a beast, many logical problems arise from it; for if it had been so, first, how would one have known how to distinguish and discern him from the others, if he had entirely changed human form and been altogether equal to the others? Moreover, the justice of God would not have been so notorious in the punishment of sin, whereas our God desires that we should use the example of others <note>A common Renaissance view that divine punishment serves as a public "mirror" or warning to others.</note>. 

<margin>**Thomas Aquinas**, Book 2 of *On the Governance of Rulers*, final chapter.</margin> Others have opined only that his imagination was so depraved that he thought himself to be entirely a beast.

To this, **Saint Gregory** seems to want to agree in the previously cited passage, saying: *Because by the pride of his thought he raised himself above men, he lost that very sense which he had in common with men*; <note>original Latin: "Quia elatione cogitationis se supra homines extulit, ipsum, quem communem cum hominibus habuit sensum, amisit"</note> because he pridefully raised himself above other men, he lost the <term>sense</term> <note>Latin: "sensum." In medieval philosophy, this refers to the "common sense" or faculty of reason that separates humans from animals.</note> that he had in common with men. These are the words of St. Gregory, which clearly show us his agreement with the saying of St. Jerome; for what is it to have a "common sense" with men, if not the understanding and use of discretion, by which men are distinguished from beasts, and of which he was deprived for such a long time? It was not, therefore, that he had been converted or transformed into a beast, seeing as the said author does not say that he had the sense common to beasts, but rather that he lost the sense he had in common with men. This is very well confirmed for us by the <term>Ordinary Gloss</term> <note>The "Glossa Ordinaria," a standard collection of biblical commentaries used by scholars in the 16th century.</note>, which says this: *He is compared to an ox because of the overturned state of his mind, and because of his hairy body, and because of the food proper to an ox*; <note>original Latin: "Boui comparatur propter statum animi euersum, & propter corpus pilosum, & propter cibum bouis proprium"</note> He is compared to the ox for

<margin>Book 2 of Exodus, chapter 21 <note>This citation refers to the biblical account of Pharaoh’s magicians competing with Moses and Aaron.</note></margin>
**Saint Augustine** is of this opinion when he specifically treats this passage, where he holds that although demons cannot create, nevertheless, knowing the proper matter to induce a <term>substantial form</term> <note>original: "forme substantielle"; a philosophical term for the essential nature that makes a thing what it is.</note>, they can apply it artificially. However, he seems to maintain that it was more in external appearance that such rods appeared to be serpents, rather than being substituted by others; and from this he brings forward this authority: *Moses’s rod was able to swallow what they were, and not what they were not* <note>original: "Absorbere potuit virga Mosis quod erant, & non quod non erant." Augustine argues that Moses's rod devoured the physical matter of the magicians' rods, proving they were still essentially wood despite their appearance.</note>. This is to say that even if the said rods were not changed, it was not impossible for God to make them be devoured in the form of wood as easily as otherwise; by which discourse you see that everything is attributed to the power of God. It was this power that first punished **Nebuchadnezzar** in such a way that—although his human form was changed neither truly nor by imagination—nevertheless, because of his brutal actions, the text uses the word "mutation" <note>original: "mutation"; here implying a change in behavior or state of mind rather than physical species.</note>. **Lot's wife** was truly changed, and **Moses’s rod** likewise, so that such examples have no place in this present discussion <note>The author argues that these were divine miracles (real physical changes), whereas lycanthropy or demonic magic involves mere illusion or behavioral shifts.</note>.

Is it [the demon] who can do that?

->FIRST SPEAKER.<-

To properly understand these two questions, which depend on one another, it is first necessary to know how many species of <term>prestiges</term> <note>In the 16th century, a "prestige" (from the Latin *praestigium*) was not a matter of social status, but a technical term for a sensory illusion, trick, or "juggling" of the eyes that makes a person see something that isn't there.</note> there are; for some can be produced by artifice and without magic, others by diabolic art, and others in another fashion. The authors of the *Malleus Maleficarum* <note>The "Hammer of Witches," a famous 15th-century treatise on the prosecution of witches.</note> therefore say that the prestigious art, or enchantment, is performed in three ways. 

First, without the help of demons, which species ought rather to be called mockery or deception than anything else, because it is done artificially through human movement, by showing or hiding certain things, just as is done in games of sleight-of-hand by <term>basteleurs</term> <note>Showmen or street performers, similar to modern stage magicians or jugglers.</note>. 

The second species of illusion, called "natural," is also performed without the help of demons, through a special virtue and force that is born and comes naturally with certain bodies—whether stones, water, wood, or other things—or which, being placed in opposition to something else, makes it appear and show itself as something other than it is. In this way, Saint Thomas and several other doctors testify to a certain herb which, when lit and beginning to smoke, makes the ceiling beams appear to be serpents. 

The third delusion or illusion encompasses all these preceding ones and several others, all of which can be performed by demons; for the first, which we have just discussed, he [the demon] can perform artificially and through jugglery, insofar as all that man knows how to do artificially,

...said state of magic) that by enchanting the said Faustinian, <note>In early Christian legends known as the *Pseudo-Clementine* writings, the sorcerer Simon Magus is said to have magically swapped his appearance with Faustinian (the father of St. Clement) to escape capture by the authorities.</note> he took it upon himself to take his likeness and appearance and give him his own, to avoid danger by this means, as he did. I will provide another example, though a humorous one, which will entirely confirm for us how not everyone is in the power of the devil, or of magicians, to be thus enchanted, and that such things may happen to us according to the faith we have in them. Bodin <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596) was a famous French jurist and demonologist. This story appears in his 1580 work, *On the Demon-Mania of Sorcerers* (*De la Démonomanie des Sorciers*).</note> relates the story of a certain Priest who, while walking on a feast day before his Church and holding his breviary <note>A book containing the daily office or prayers required to be recited by Catholic clergy.</note> in his hands, was approached by the sorcerer Trois-échelles. <note>Trois-échelles (literally "Three-ladders") was a notorious sorcerer executed in 1574 during the reign of Charles IX; he reportedly confessed to the existence of thousands of witches in France.</note> The sorcerer, wanting to provide a laugh for the company, began with a verbal reprimand to reproach him, saying it was a great shame for a Pastor like himself to publicly carry and hold a deck of cards on a feast day and in a holy place. He pressed the matter so convincingly that the said Priest was persuaded by it, so that when he looked at his own hands, he perceived that it was true. As a sign of his indignation, he took his said breviary and threw it right into the middle of the mud, walking away quite ashamed. But a young man who was there, perceiving it to be nothing other than a breviary and not being fooled by such a dazzlement or charm, <term>esbloüissemẽt</term> <note>This term refers to a "bewilderment" of the senses, a common explanation in the Renaissance for how magicians made people see things that weren't there.</note> immediately picked up the breviary and returned it to the priest.

It is to teach us how not everyone is always enchanted, and [how things] sometimes occur and happen according to the faith we place in them. I will propose a few more examples for more ample proof. Antoninus <note>Saint Antoninus of Florence (1389–1459), a Dominican friar and Archbishop who wrote the *Summa Historialis*, a massive chronicle of world history.</note> in the second part, namely in the life of Saint Germain, <note>Saint Germain of Auxerre (c. 378–448), a Gaulish bishop known for his travels and miracles.</note> relates that the said holy personage, traveling in a foreign land, also noticed strange things. ### ON LYCANTHROPY.

->SCIPION.<-

What is the reason why this can happen to us, and why the evil spirit, or sorcerers, have such power over man?

->PROTERON.<-

The reason is assigned to the last example mentioned, for Saint Macarius <note>Saint Macarius of Egypt (c. 300–391 AD), a desert father credited with many miracles involving the dispelling of demonic illusions.</note> told the aforementioned woman that she had suffered such an illusion for the space of five weeks because she had not frequented the Holy Sacraments; for which reason he enjoined her never to separate herself from holy communion and prayer. It is also very certain that he <note>The devil or the "evil spirit."</note> does not have as much power—even for such illusions—against Christians as he has against pagans. And I may well say, along with many doctors of the church, that these "prestiges" <note>The term *prestiges* refers to supernatural optical illusions or "juggling" of the senses by demons, making a person appear to be an animal.</note> can never, or rarely, occur in a country except when it abandons God and His religion, for then the devil has greater power there.

->SCIPION.<-

So then, he who might be deprived of such blessings, or who even, being constrained by necessity, had not assisted at Mass that day, could he not have recourse to other weapons or protection? Little children, by this means, would be in danger, having no way to defend themselves.

<meta>catchword: -cations,</meta>

Besides the aforementioned weapons, those which are most suitable and most ready at hand to break the forces of our enemies, both visible and invisible, is the sign of the cross. For just as a fearful and timid person cannot endure a lightning bolt, so too this cowardly and craven Satan—though he seems to boldly assail us—needs only a single sign of the cross to be terrified and put to flight. This is for no other reason than that Jesus Christ triumphant, by such weapons, has overthrown his enemies and ours, according to the testimony of that ancient and Catholic poet Juvencus <note>Gaius Vettius Aquilinus Juvencus (fl. 4th century AD), a Spanish priest and one of the earliest Christian Latin poets.</note>,

<margin>In the Triumph of Christ. Heroic verse.</margin>
> *He himself placed the cross of the tree, that venerable wood,* <note>original: "Arboris ipse crucem posuit venerabile lignum,"</note>
> *From whose broken branches, with necks shattered,*
> *The bound enemies hang with their eyes swollen.*

He set up the cross, a wooden tree worthy of honor and veneration; from its branches and boughs his enemies are attached, hanging with swollen eyes, and their heads (that is to say, their authority and power) broken. 

<margin>Ambrose, Sermon 47. Chrysostom, Sermon on the Cross and Baptism.</margin>
From this comes the fact that the Church Fathers <note>Specifically referring to St. Ambrose and St. John Chrysostom, as noted in the margin.</note> exhort and persuade us to always be fortified with such a shield and safeguard. <margin>Question on Witches <note>original Latin: "q. de Stry." refers to *Quaestio de Strigibus* (A Question on Witches), a work by Bartolomeo de Spina.</note></margin>
Bartolomeo de Spina <note>Bartolomeo de Spina (c. 1475–1546) was an Italian Dominican theologian and Inquisitor who wrote extensively on the legal and theological aspects of witchcraft.</note> recounts the story of a certain witch, who confessed to having been more than fifty times by night in the house of a neighbor and relative (for these are the ones they most often target, especially when there is some familiarity) with the intention of killing a small child in the cradle. <margin>*The Hammer of Witches*, Part 2, Question 1, Chapter 2. <note>original: "Mal. M p. 2. q. 1. c. 2." refers to the *Malleus Maleficarum*, the most famous and influential treatise on the prosecution of witches, first published in 1486.</note></margin>
that in a short time thirteen children had been devoured, for which reason a diligent search was made to discover how this could happen. Now it happened that among others, one who was questioned <note>The OCR "surrogée" likely refers to an interrogation during a trial.</note> on this point, declared at great length not only that they had been put to death by them, but furthermore, why and how. ...against the human race, he never ceases to seek prey there, even against children—either by making them perish in the mother's womb, or by arranging for them to be offered to him in the form of a sacrifice by midwives (if they are witches) immediately after the said children are come into the world and before baptism; or else after, by having them charmed and enchanted by a look or a kiss from a witch. In short, although all men are subject to many miseries, for this reason children have even more to suffer. This is what Saint Augustine greatly deplored when he said:

<margin>Book 21 of the *City of God*, chapter 18.</margin>

*“Indeed, it is written: a heavy yoke is upon the sons of Adam, from the day of their departure from their mother’s womb until the day of their burial. This is fulfilled to such an extent that even little children, though they are already released from the bond of original sin—by which alone they were held—through the <term>washing of rebirth; baptism</term>, suffer many things, and some even endure the attacks of malicious spirits.”* <note>original Latin: "Prorsusque scriptum est: grave iugum super filios Adam, a die exitus de ventre matris eorum, vsque in diem sepulturæ adeo impleri necesse est; vt ipsi paruuli per lauacrum regenerationis ab originali peccato, quo solum tenebantur, vinculo iam soluti; multa patientes, nonnulli incursus malignorum spirituum patiantur." This quotes Ecclesiasticus 40:1 as cited by Augustine.</note> Such a grievous subjection is fixed in this manner against the children of Adam, and must necessarily be fulfilled from the day of their birth until the hour of death; so much so that even little children, being already freed from original sin (by which alone they were bound) by the washing of baptism, still do not cease to suffer much thereafter, so that some even endure the incursions of evil spirits.

->### DIALOGUE I.<-

...so much <note>The text continues from the previous page: "so much so that..."</note> [that] when great or small are signed with the sign of the cross, neither devil nor **sorcerer** has any power, for it is the greatest enemy they can have, and one which immediately puts them to flight. This is why, among the protestations that the devil requires from these **sorcerers** or **magicians** (among which there are three principal ones, namely: to renounce **Christianity**, never to adore the **Eucharist**, and to trample the cross underfoot), wherever they can secretly find the cross, they not only fail to show it the due reverence, but what is worse, they set their foot upon it. 

And I believe that what makes the cross so detested in these times is not only **heresy** (which many claim as their excuse), but rather the protestation and oath they have made to sooner adore a stinking goat <note>A common historical reference to the devil's appearance at a Witches' Sabbath.</note> than to recognize this life-giving sign. Thus, **heresy** and magic are closely related, since every **magician** is a heretic, or at least an **idolater**; and this is especially true when he adores the devil, whether with the adoration of <term>latria; the supreme worship due to God alone</term> or even of <term>dulia; the veneration accorded to saints and angels</term>. 

But what? Alas! We speak here of the sign of the cross, which is the means of dispelling <term>illusions; deceptive demonic appearances or "prestiges"</term> and **sorceries**, and which is such an enemy to them and their ways; yet we seek them out every day, or very often. We would like, when we are in prosperity, not to think of such wickedness, but if we have so much as a pain in the tip of a finger, suddenly we have recourse to such wicked vermin rather than to God himself, or indeed...

For superstition is the direct path both to Magic and to infidelity or idolatry.

->ELEION.<-

What do you call superstition?

->PROTERON.<-

<margin>Summa Theologiae, II-II, question 97, article 4, and question 94, article 1.</margin>
Superstition, as Saint Thomas <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), a hugely influential philosopher and theologian whose "Summa Theologiae" defined much of Catholic doctrine.</note> says, is an actual protestation of infidelity. And in article 94, he cites the gloss <note>A "gloss" is a brief explanatory note or interpretation in the margin or between lines of a text, particularly the Bible.</note> on the second chapter of Colossians, calling it a human tradition which takes the name of religion.

<margin>On True Religion, chapter 4. On the City of God, book 4, chapter 30.</margin>
Saint Augustine <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian theologian whose works on the "City of God" and "Christian Doctrine" are cited here.</note> calls it honor paid to demons; and in another place he names it the contrary of religion. Finally, we can say that anything that diminishes the honor and glory of God, under a pretext of piety or religion, is truly superstition.

->SCIPION.<-

Such as what?

->PROTERON.<-

<margin>Book 2 of On Christian Doctrine, chapter 20.</margin>
There are five types described by Saint Augustine in the second book of *On Christian Doctrine*, namely: idolatry, divination, undue honor to God, and the vain observation of things, in which are included all the branches of superstition. But since not everyone could comprehend or understand this division, we will try to make it clear and plain for you briefly through five rules. The

The first of which is that in all works concerning the Christian religion, one must consider whether their end and principal goal are for the honor and glory of God, following even in this the sentence of St. Paul who says: <margin>1 Corinthians 10</margin> *Whether we eat, drink, or whatever else we do, let it redound to the glory of God.* <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 10:31: "Whether therefore ye eat, or drink, or whatsoever ye do, do all to the glory of God."</note> For if one intends another goal, the end result will be worthless. The third is to see if the work one does has the natural virtue and property to produce the effect one expects; for otherwise it is superstitious. <page-num>69</page-num>
-># ON LYCANTHROPY.<-

...is shaken off, and the yoke of the Church is cast down; one pays little heed to its commandments or prohibitions, especially a pack of half-lost souls who, believing in their own foolish judgment and brains, take no account of anything the Church might tell them. I have strayed somewhat from our subject, but you have been able to think meanwhile about the types of superstition, and how great they are.

### SCIPION.

You have, it seems to me, criticized those who wear <term>amulets</term> <note>original: "breuets." These were small slips of paper or parchment inscribed with holy names or magic symbols, often sewn into clothing for protection.</note>, including them in the third type; I would nevertheless like to be even better enlightened on this. For it was not long ago that a certain soldier, having been condemned by his Captain to pass through the arms and be <term>arquebused</term> <note>original: "harquebouzé." An early form of execution by firing squad using the arquebus, a heavy portable matchlock gun.</note>, was only put to death with great difficulty; for no other reason than for having sewn upon himself an amulet of the **high names** <note>original: "hauts noms." Likely referring to the mystical or secret names of God used in the Kabbalah or Solomonic magic to invoke protection.</note>.

### ELEION.

There are, besides those, still others who, by a certain virtue residing in their weapons, on their bodies, in their clothes, or otherwise, cannot be wounded at all—one in the head, another in some part of his body; I wish to know where they might get such virtue, and if it is superstition?

### PROTERON.

These two questions are entirely comprised within superstition. It is quite true that the last one is more pernicious and wicked, for...

<meta>catchword: gene- (likely beginning the word "generalement" or "generations")</meta>

<summary>In this dialogue, the characters discuss the use of "breuets" (amulets) and magical charms used by soldiers to achieve invulnerability against firearms and other weapons. They categorize these practices as dangerous forms of superstition that defy the authority of the Church.</summary>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the response of Proteron regarding the nature of superstitious protections against injury. It follows the interrupted sentence from the previous page: "It is quite true that..."</meta>

<page-num>218</page-num>
<header>DIALOGUE</header>

...generally speaking, whoever practices it is a **magician** or a **juggler**; <note>original: "baſteleur." This term implies a street performer or charlatan, suggesting that such magic is often viewed as low-class trickery or deceptive performance.</note> but the other form is not always evil. As for this specific kind, it is performed in two ways: either by enchanting the weapons of those who wish to do them harm (and this with certain charms <note>original: "carmes," referring to incantations or verses derived from the Latin "carmina."</note> pronounced on the spot) or even by enchanting their own, such that they can dance upon the sharp edges of them. 

The other way is by offering insults and injuries to images, or breaking them, in order to obtain what they ask of the devil through this means. As, for example, he who does not wish to be wounded in the head will take the head of such an image; he who desires not to be struck in the arm will break an arm off it, and so on for all parts of the body. And this is why one rarely finds a whole image at crossroads or in public places. 

Such **iconoclasts** <term>iconoclasts</term> <note>Literally "image-breakers." While usually referring to those who destroy religious icons for theological reasons, here it describes a form of "sympathetic magic" where damaging a statue is believed to protect the corresponding part of the magician's own body.</note> and image-breakers, joining and associating themselves with malevolent archers (who, in order to kill a certain number of men on **Good Friday** during the divine service, shoot as many arrows or darts into the image of the **Crucifix** as the number of men they intend to kill) are truly placed in the rank of **apostates** and **excommunicated**, even though they do it only for the preservation of their bodies, whereas **Magicians** do it to the damage of others.

<margin>Treatise on superstition.</margin>
Now as for the other kind, which concerns **slips** or **charms**, <note>original: "billets ou breuets." These were small pieces of paper or parchment with sacred or magical words written on them, worn for protection.</note> it is not only recently that they began; for it is an ancient error of the pagans, such that **Plutarch** was scandalized by it in his time; "The superstitious man," he said, "will grieve to have in [little...]"

<summary>This page describes two forms of superstitious protection used by soldiers and others: the enchanting of weapons and the ritual mutilation of religious statues (sympathetic magic) to ensure personal invulnerability. It categorizes these practitioners as apostates and links the practice of wearing written charms to ancient pagan traditions.</summary>

# ON LYCANTHROPY.

<page-num>70</page-num>

...thing having offended the Gods, and in this state of sadness, old women will come to attach and hang around his neck—no more nor less than onto a stake driven into the ground—all the charms, <note>original: "breuets." These were written amulets or slips of paper containing "magical" words or prayers, often worn for protection.</note> sorceries, and follies they possess. The opinion of St. Augustine on this is as follows: <margin>Book on Christian Doctrine; also found in Decree 26, Question 2, Chapter *Illud*. Also Isidore, *Etymologies*, Book 8, Chapter 9.</margin> *To superstition belong a thousand magical arts, as well as bindings <term>ligatures</term> <note>Magical "bindings" or "ligatures" (from Latin *ligatura*) were charms believed to physically or metaphorically bind a person, spirit, or disease to prevent harm or cause impotence.</note> and remedies, which even the discipline of doctors condemns, whether in incantations, or in certain marks they call "characters," or in certain objects to be hung and designated.* <note>original: "Ad superstitionem pertinent mille magicarum artium & ligaturae & remedia..."</note> To superstition belong a thousand ties and bindings of the magical arts, and remedies which are rejected by the science of physicians, whether in the form of enchantments, or in certain notes that they call "characters," or indeed in certain things to be hung up and marked. St. Chrysostom says no less of it, even when he speaks of the charms that might be granted; I will set it down here in the common tongue <note>The author is translating Chrysostom's Greek/Latin into the vernacular (French) for his readers; I have provided it here in English.</note> because it is quite lengthy. "Some," he says, "carry a portion of the written Gospel around their necks; but one must ask: is the Gospel not read every day in the Church, and is it not heard by everyone? How then, being hung around the neck, can these words protect a man when they have profited him nothing despite ringing in his ears? Furthermore, where does the force and virtue of the Gospel lie? Does it lie in the shapes and characters of the letters, or rather in the meaning? If you believe it lies in the shapes, you are right to put them around your neck; but if you believe it lies in the meaning of the words, you will consequently believe that they will profit you more if they are placed within your heart." Thus far are the words of St. Chrysostom, who not—

<meta>catchword: ob-</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the use of physical amulets and written charms (breuets), citing St. Augustine and St. Chrysostom to argue that true spiritual protection comes from the heart and understanding, not from objects hung around the neck.</summary>

For this reason, they wish to teach us to rather lift our intention toward God and toward the meaning of the charm, <note>original: "breuet." A small written slip or amulet, often containing prayers or names of saints, worn on the person.</note> than to the characters which are found therein. And thus St. Thomas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Dominican friar and philosopher whose *Summa Theologica* defined much of Catholic doctrine.</note> interprets it; therefore, keeping the conditions that the same doctor adds in this place, it shall be permitted to use them. The first is such that they be not for the invocation of devils; the second, that they contain no unknown names; the third, that they contain nothing of falsehood; the fourth, that there be no signs or characters, save and except the sign of the cross, nor any thing of vanity mixed with the holy things which are therein; the fifth is that, whether in writing it, or attaching it, or wearing it, one observes nothing of

<margin>*Armilla Aurea*.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding superstition and charms. The catchword "pren-" from the previous page completes the phrase regarding what practices to "take up" or "undertake." The speakers are summarizing their previous debate on the nature of werewolves (lycanthropy).</meta>

# ON LYCANTHROPY.

...take up now. We shall bring an end to our discourse, whenever it seems good to you.

->SCIPION.<-

It would still seem good to me to first make a collection and summary of all the above, and even to resolve it by articles, to better imprint it in the memory.

->ELEION.<-

It seems very good and profitable to me.

->PROTERON.<-

The first resolution shall be, therefore, that for sin and the punishment thereof, irrational animals <note>original: "animaux irraiſonables," referring to beasts as they lack the human faculty of reason.</note> avenging their Creator, by His permission, and sometimes by express command, assail men to inflict punishment.
The second, that these may be natural wolves, which are seen thus devouring men; doing so either by the rage <note>original: "rage," likely referring to rabies.</note> in which they are; by the hunger by which they are pressed; by the agitation of devils; or other reasons stated above.
The 3rd, that these may be natural men, who either without charms or with charms, live on human flesh, as we have proved by examples.
The 4th, that neither Lycanthropy, nor real transformation, is admitted in any way in the Catholic Church; and that if man is transformed (if it is nonetheless permissible to use the word), it is rather in good than in evil, and not to transform or change into a beast, seeing that neither ointments,

<summary>Proteron provides a four-point summary of the dialogue’s conclusions on lycanthropy, arguing that while animals may punish humans by God's will, the Catholic Church denies the possibility of a literal physical transformation of a human into a wolf.</summary>
<keywords>lycanthropy, werewolves, Catholic doctrine, divine punishment, rabies, cannibalism, metamorphosis, ointments</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the resolution of the dialogue on lycanthropy. The previous page ended with the statement that no physical transformation into a beast is possible via ointments; this page continues that list of ineffective magical means (rivers, words, belts) and concludes the six points of the argument.</meta>

<header>DIALOGUE</header>

nor rivers, nor words, nor <term>belts</term> <note>In folklore, a magical belt or girdle made of wolf-skin was often said to be the tool used to transform a human into a werewolf.</note>, nor the devil himself have any power to do it; rather, they can only make people appear externally other than they are, and persuade themselves of it—even hiding the human form and figure by enchantment so that one does not know what sort of people they are. But one cannot admit such transformation unless, as a consequence, one also admits either the Pythagorean <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>The philosophical belief in the transmigration of the soul into a new body, human or animal, after death.</note>, or the *Metamorphoses* of Ovid, and other such fabulous absurdities.

<margin>
Jean Bodin,
*On the Demon-mania of Sorcerers*,
Book on the
Examination of
Lycanthropes.
<note>original: "D. Ioan. Bodin. pi Ruuemu- logia, Deca- uamij, Lib examinati Lycãthropi"</note>
</margin>
The 5th resolution is that, according to the faith placed in it, men through magic, or devils within an assumed body, can make themselves—or others—be seen in a strange form. They do this by enchanting or dazzling both the sight and the other senses, both internal and external.

<margin>
Acquiesce to
Genebrard
<note>Likely referring to Gilbert Génébrard, a 16th-century theologian.</note>
With the
Minister
Arnold.
</margin>
The 6th and last resolution is that by detesting and rejecting all charms and all superstition, and by using the blessings of the Church (and especially the sign of the cross), anyone, whether great or small, who is marked by them can not only avoid such sorcery and enchantments, but also the danger that might follow. For the devil has no power over the true faithful, whom Jesus Christ preserves, triumphing (through the cross) over His enemies and ours. Let us therefore always be careful to furnish and arm both those in our charge and ourselves with this glorious sign of the cross. Let us take up this <term>staff of Jacob</term> <note>original: "bourdon de Iacob." A "bourdon" is a pilgrim's staff; the reference is to Genesis 32:10, symbolizing spiritual support and God's protection during a journey.</note> to support ourselves as we cross the ford of the waters of bitterness of this mortal life, which is agitated by so many mis[eries...]

<summary>The author concludes the argument against physical lycanthropy, labeling it a pagan absurdity, and instead attributes the phenomenon to demonic sensory deception. The final resolution encourages the use of the sign of the cross as a spiritual defense against such enchantments.</summary>
<keywords>metempsychosis, Jean Bodin, Ovid, sensory deception, sign of the cross, Jacob's staff, spiritual protection, demonic illusion</keywords>

## APPROVAL. <note>original: "CENSVRA." In the 16th century, a "censura" was an official review by church authorities to ensure a book contained no heresy before it was granted a license to be printed.</note>

By the mandate and commission of the Most Reverend Lord, Lord Henry Cuyckius <note>Henricus Cuyckius (1546–1609) was a Dutch bishop and scholar who served as a censor of books in Louvain.</note>, Bishop-elect of Roermond <note>original: "Ruremunden."</note>, Chancellor of the Faculty of Theology, Dean of the Collegiate Church of Saint Peter in Louvain, and Censor of Books, I have visited, read, and examined the present book, *Dialogue on Lycanthropy, or the transformation of men into wolves, and whether such a thing can be done,* by Brother Claude Prieur, in which, finding nothing contrary to the Catholic or orthodox faith, I judge that it may be profitably and fruitfully published.
->*Brother Egidius Cheheré, Professor of Sacred Theology.*<-

I concur with the approval of the excellent Master Egidius Cheheré, Professor of Sacred Theology; and I judge that the above-written book may be printed.

->*Henry Cuyckius, Dean of Saint Peter in Louvain,*<-
->*and Papal and Royal*<-
->*Censor of books.*<-

With the consent of the Reverend Father Gerard Jaceanus, Provincial Minister, and the venerable father Arnold of Ischa, Guardian of Louvain.

## REVIEW. <note>original: "CENSURA." In the early modern period, a "censure" was a formal theological evaluation required before a book could receive a printing license, ensuring it contained no heresy.</note>

By the mandate of the Most Illustrious and Most Reverend Lord, Lord Diego de Tejada <note>Diego de Tejada y la Guardia (1601–1663) was the Bishop of Pamplona during the mid-17th century.</note>, Bishop of Pamplona, etc., I have attentively read, read through, and examined this book, entitled: *Pastoral Instructions*, etc., written by the Most Illustrious and Most Reverend Lord, Lord Francis de Sales <note>St. Francis de Sales (1567–1622), a Doctor of the Church, was renowned for his deep spirituality and his role as the Bishop of Geneva.</note>, formerly Bishop and Prince of Geneva, and composed in the French language by the Most Illustrious and Most Reverend Lord, Lord André de Saussay <note>André de Saussay (1595–1659) was a French bishop and scholar who wrote extensively on ecclesiastical history and succeeded the Sales family in the See of Geneva.</note>, likewise Bishop and Prince of Geneva; in which I have found nothing that is contrary to the orthodox faith or good morals: but rather I judge it most useful for the advancement of souls and most worthy of all praise.

->At Pamplona, on the 5th day of July, 1656.<-

In the authentic copy there stands [the name of] the Very Reverend Father Brother Giles of Saint Teresa, Discalced Carmelite <note>The Discalced Carmelites are a reform branch of the Carmelite Order, known for their strict observance of poverty and contemplative prayer.</note>, and written and signed by his own hand, just as it appears in the original itself.

->I, Abbot Lord Peter Joseph, Dean and Commissioner of the Holy Inquisition <note>The Holy Inquisition was the ecclesiastical tribunal tasked with maintaining religious orthodoxy and regulating the publication of books.</note>, etc., bear witness.<-

---

With the consent of the Most Illustrious and Most Reverend Lord, Lord Diego de Tejada, Bishop of Pamplona, etc., given to the press in this Convent of Discalced Carmelites on the 11th day of July in the year 1656.
